Top Traders Unplugged 1 episode(s) · 40 insights

Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore | Systematic Investor | Ep.41277 min40 insights2026-08-09
▶ Watch on YouTube
Man Group's Harry Moore and host Niels Kaastrup-Larsen use the July 2026 AI-trade unwind (a hedge fund that reached ~$45bn before losing two-thirds) and the yen intervention to argue that most multi-asset books are a single concentrated bet, then walk through the portable-alpha paper Moore co-wrote: hold beta via futures/swaps, keep ~40% cash (10% margin plus 30% unencumbered) to avoid being forcibly degeared, rebalance slowly with tolerance bands, and pick an alpha that is uncorrelated in the left tail. They close on the mathematics-of-turtle-trading paper (range-based vol, unit sizing, 2N stops as fractional Kelly) and on AI collapsing the implementation half of quant research.
AI-trade concentration and left-tail portfolio resilience · 6Yen intervention and unsimulatable tail moves · 2July-August tape: model speed, sector legs and market universe · 7Portable alpha cash buffers and knockout risk · 6Portable alpha rebalancing, instruments and alpha selection · 5Leverage as a portfolio-construction tool · 4Turtle rules as early decision theory · 6AI inside quant research and the junior hiring problem · 4
AI-trade concentration and left-tail portfolio resilience 6
A hedge fund built around Leopold Ashenbrenner's AI thesis gathered roughly $45 billion in assets on a very short track record and then lost about two-thirds of it in a matter of weeks in July 2026.
data_point🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host)conviction: highLeopold AshenbrennerSituational AwarenessAI tradehedge funds
So what: A $45bn single-theme vehicle unwinding in weeks is a live warning that AI-theme crowding, not valuation alone, is the near-term source of forced-selling risk in tech.
“here someone with very little experience, very little track record um you know ends up having $45 billion or so uh under management before losing um 2/3 of that it seems in in a matter of weeks” ▶ 4:27
A book can look dollar-neutral or market-neutral and still be a concentrated directional bet if both legs are expressions of the same theme, which is how the AI-trade fund was hedged.
causal🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highAI trademarket neutralhedge funds
So what: Screen alpha managers on thematic exposure, not just gross/net and beta neutrality; theme concentration is the exposure that actually kills the P&L.
“even if on paper you look somewhat like dollar neutral or market neutral, your your your theme is is is has kind of caught you out there”
Moore deliberately holds both AI scenarios open: the railroad/dotcom capex-mania analogy that ends in tears, and an upside case in which the trade that broke in July 2026 turns out to be the right trade for the next ten years.
thesis🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumAIrailroadsdotcom bustcapexMan Group
So what: If the honest prior is genuine two-sided uncertainty, position sizing and convexity matter more than picking the AI bubble's top.
“Maybe there is this huge upside scenario where um the trade that we saw go wrong last month is the right trade and it works over over the next 10 years.” ▶ 24:06
A conventional allocator book of equities, bonds, commodities and private assets is effectively one long bet on continued AI capex and growth, and in a 30-40% equity drawdown none of those sleeves protects the portfolio.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highequitiesbondsprivate assetscommoditiesAI capex
So what: Stress the whole book against a 30-40% index decline rather than a 5-10% wobble; the diversification that shows up in normal-times correlations disappears exactly when it is needed.
“on paper, you look diversified, right? These assets they seem to have kind of in normal times somewhat low correlations with each other.” ▶ 25:04
Bonds and gold both sold off in correlated fashion earlier in 2026, and with US inflation running above target for years the Fed may lack room to cut aggressively, so bonds could sell off hard in the same shock that hits equities.
causal🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumFederal Reservebondsgoldinflation
So what: The 60/40 rate-cut cushion is conditional on the Fed having room; an inflation-constrained Fed makes duration a poor equity hedge and raises the value of price-following diversifiers.
“inflation's now been running above target for many many years. It's not just like it's it's it's a short period. It's it's many years. do they have the tools to really cut rates” ▶ 26:28
Trend following earns its place in an AI-concentrated book because it holds no long-term view, can flip to short bonds and short equities, go long commodities in an inflation shock, and can even short the AI theme itself by selling semis and tech hardware names.
thesis🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: hightrend followingsemiconductorstech hardwarecommoditiesbonds
So what: The relevant test for a diversifier is the covariance matrix in the shock, not the full-sample correlation, and trend is typically zero-to-negatively correlated there.
“as an investor when you look and you say okay my assets are are uncorrelated but are they uncorrelated in that left tail shock like what does your coariance matrix look like in that shock” ▶ 28:24
Yen intervention and unsimulatable tail moves 2
Japanese (and possibly US) authorities intervened in the yen only days before July 2026 month-end, and the move showed up in managed-futures performance for the month.
data_point🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host)conviction: highBank of JapanJapanese yenScott BessentCTAs
So what: Short-yen was the crowded trend position, so July manager dispersion is largely explained by yen sizing and speed rather than skill.
“the central banks of Japan and possibly I guess the US as well intervening uh in the Japanese yen only a couple of days before month end” ▶ 6:14
The yen intervention move was roughly a 10-standard-deviation event, against the 20-30 standard deviations of the Swiss franc de-peg, and such policy-driven moves cannot be reproduced by simulated market data.
data_point🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host) and Harry Mooreconviction: highJapanese yenSwiss francSNBrisk models
So what: Backtests and Monte Carlo engines systematically understate intervention risk, so single-position caps rather than model-implied VaR are the real defence, and rules-based systems that hold through the rumour phase must size for it.
“That that that does not appear in if you're if you're building market data that's very hard to to actually simulate something like that. But we we know they exist from from time to time.”
July-August tape: model speed, sector legs and market universe 7
As of 11 August 2026 the BTOP50 was down 67bp in the month and up 7.17% YTD, the SG CTA index down 88bp and up 7.15%, the SG Trend index down 94bp and up 6.89%, while the short-term traders index was up 14bp in August but only 3.32% YTD.
data_point🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host)conviction: highBTOP50SG CTA IndexSG Trend IndexSG Short-Term Traders Index
So what: Trend is up ~7% YTD with a soft start to August, and the short-term sub-strategy is lagging badly, confirming the slow-over-fast regime.
“It's still up 7.17% so far this year. The sock gen CG index is down 88 basis points up 7.15% so far this year.” ▶ 13:08
Traditional benchmarks were far stronger in early August 2026: MSCI World up 2.77% in the month and 12.45% YTD, MSCI World ex-US/Canada up 1.83% and 11.8%, the S&P US aggregate bond index up 64bp in the month but only 31bp YTD, and the S&P 500 total return up 3.12% in the month and 12.83% YTD.
data_point🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host)conviction: highMSCI WorldS&P 500S&P US Aggregate Bond Index
So what: Equities are compounding while bonds are flat YTD, so the diversification burden in 2026 books sits entirely on non-bond diversifiers.
July 2026 dispersion among trend managers came from the split between rallying energy markets (profitable) and the stalled AI trade, where long semis and tech hardware against short software hurt, so asset-class risk weights and market exclusions determined the month's outcome.
causal🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highcrude oilsemiconductorssoftwaretrend followingenergy
So what: Attribute July manager returns to energy weight and equity-sector exposure rather than to trend skill when doing manager selection.
“choices about how much to have within each asset class, how much risk weight to have in that in the long run, choices of whether to exclude certain markets or whole asset classes would have created a lot a lot of dispersion in in July” ▶ 10:41
Running one standard trend model across four lookbacks shows a 20-day speed down YTD in 2026, a 60-day speed down but less, a 130-day speed slightly up and a 260-day speed up the most.
data_point🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host)conviction: hightrend followingmoving average lookbacks
So what: Speed is the single biggest explanator of 2026 trend dispersion; allocators should ask managers for their effective lookback before judging performance.
“if you had chosen a 20-day look back, again, this is just from this specific model, um, you would actually have been down uh, this year.” ▶ 15:24
Slow trend models won in 2026 because they stayed persistently short bonds, held long energy positions longer and stayed long equities throughout, and no turning point has persisted long enough for fast models to shine.
causal🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highbondsenergyequitiestrend following
So what: The slow-outperforms regime has run several years now, but Moore still argues for dynamic speed so the book can capture the eventual turning point.
“we haven't had a a a turning point that that's that's persisted for long enough for for for the faster speeds to to kind of shine” ▶ 16:56
Alternative markets underperformed traditional ones in 2026: Nordic and Italian power stayed rangebound while mid-frequency models made money in Brent and heating oil, and alternative rates such as Polish interest-rate swaps gyrated more than 10-year Treasuries or gilts.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highNordic powerItalian powerBrent crudeheating oilPolish interest rate swaps10-year Treasurygilts
So what: The alternative-markets premium that justified higher fees has been a drag this year, so compare managers on market universe before attributing 2026 gaps to skill.
“those markets have have just tended to be a little bit more rangebound than we've seen in the more traditional energy markets this year”
Cross-sectional equity models on cash sectors and factors (semis versus software, value, momentum, quality) were profitable in 2026 but in a completely different way to index trend, with the quality factor itself struggling year to date.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumquality factorvaluemomentumsemiconductorssoftwarecross-sectional trend
So what: Cross-sectional equity alpha is a genuinely separate return stream from index trend and should be sized as a diversifier rather than a substitute.
“quality has really struggled this year on a year-to- date basis” ▶ 20:57
Portable alpha cash buffers and knockout risk 6
Because trend runs on futures, an investor needs only about $20-25 of capital to obtain $100 of trend exposure, so adding trend does not require selling down an existing portfolio.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: hightrend followingfuturescapital efficiency
So what: The 'what do I sell to fund it' objection to trend allocations is largely a misunderstanding of margin requirements; the real constraint is governance, not cash.
“if I only need to use $20 or $25 to get my $100 of trend following gives me a few new levers and things that I can do with the freed up cash” ▶ 36:24
The portable alpha structure in Man Group's paper sells no equity at all: passive index exposure is held via a future or swap and a trend allocation is layered on top, aiming to deliver the index plus alpha.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alphaS&P 500SG Trend IndexswapsfuturesMan Group
So what: Framing trend as an overlay rather than a substitute removes the benchmark-relative career risk that blocks most trend allocations.
“I'm going to sell essentially no equity exposure, right? I'm going to keep my portfolio exactly as it was.” ▶ 38:58
Beta held on derivatives can be supported with as little as $10 of capital per $100 of exposure, freeing $90 for alpha, but that is precisely the configuration that caused portable alpha structures to fail in 2008 through insufficient liquidity and alpha-beta correlation.
causal🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alpha2008 GFCmarginliquidity
So what: Maximum capital efficiency in the structure is exactly what converts a market drawdown into forced deleveraging; the 2008 failure modes have not been engineered away.
“we know that in 2008 many of these structures failed right and the lines down which those structures failed those risks still exist” ▶ 44:31
Simulating an S&P-plus-trend portable alpha structure with a 20% cash buffer over the past 26-27 years shows the buffer fully drawn down three times: in the dotcom bust, the GFC and COVID.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alphadotcom bustGFCCOVIDS&P 500SG Trend Index
So what: A 20% buffer means being forced out of beta roughly once a decade, which is a design flaw rather than a tail event.
“that gets fully drawn down three times” ▶ 47:22
Being knocked out of the beta leg is costly twice over: an allocator goes from 100% to perhaps 20% index exposure creating massive tracking error, and de-gearing at the early-2009 low would have missed roughly 29-30% of the subsequent upside.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alpha2009 equity reboundtracking errorS&P 500
So what: Quantifying forced-degearing as ~30% of foregone rebound turns the cash-buffer decision from an operational detail into the dominant driver of long-run outcomes.
“if you were were selling in the early part of 2009 at a at a low in your equity component and you're knocked out that position, you miss kind of 29 30% of of upside” ▶ 48:14
The paper concludes that roughly 40% cash supporting the beta leg (10% margin plus 30% unencumbered) reduces forced de-gearing to about a one-in-100 to one-in-200-year event, validated with Monte Carlo rather than the single realised market path.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alphaMonte Carlo simulationmargincash bufferMan Group
So what: This is the concrete design number for anyone building or buying a portable alpha sleeve: 40% cash against beta, not the 10-20% that maximises alpha capacity.
“in the paper we end up with essentially 40% cash to support your beta. So that's 10% margin, 30% unencumbered” ▶ 48:48
Portable alpha rebalancing, instruments and alpha selection 5
Choosing a monthly rebalance between the beta and alpha legs means the structure stops replicating the index, because selling into a rising index breaks compounding and produces year-end tracking error investors do not expect.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alphaS&P 500rebalancingtracking error
So what: Anyone promising index-plus-alpha on a monthly rebalance is quietly delivering something other than the index; ask the provider to show realised tracking error.
“If it goes to 110 and you sell $10 of it to rebalance, you're no longer tracking the index.” ▶ 51:54
In 2008 every rebalancing rule other than buy-and-hold underperformed because rebalancing repeatedly bought falling equities with the proceeds of rising trend, yet over the long run the choice of monthly, quarterly, annual or 5-10% tolerance bands makes almost no performance difference.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alpha2008 GFCCOVIDrebalancing
So what: Rebalancing frequency is a path-risk decision, not a return decision, so pick the rule by which crisis paths you can tolerate, not by backtest average.
“essentially all rules other than a buy and hold underperform in 2008” ▶ 53:23
Man Group's practical recommendation is not pure buy-and-hold (which drifts and complicates subscriptions at NAV) but slow rebalancing with a sensible tolerance band, such as rebalancing only when beta and alpha drift more than 10% out of line.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumportable alphaSG Trend IndexS&P 500rebalancingNAV
So what: Tolerance-band rebalancing is the implementable middle ground that keeps index tracking tight without creating drift problems for pooled vehicles.
“if you can let that that beta replicate closely, so rebalance a little bit less, but have some sensible tolerance”
The swap-versus-future choice in portable alpha has no structural performance advantage: a bank's explicit ~40bp swap spread is implicit in the future and washes out over time, leaving operational preference and counterparty risk as the deciding factors.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highswapsfuturesportable alphacounterparty risk
So what: Do not pay for an instrument-selection 'edge' in portable alpha; negotiate on operations, collateral terms and counterparty exposure instead.
“whereas there's an explicit cost in the swap it's implicit in the future And if you run this over time, it washes out, right? The you don't get a a a structural performance advantage.”
A good portable alpha engine must be super liquid so the structure can be recapitalised quickly, which is why trend is a natural candidate, and most institutional investors want the manager to absorb the margin and cash-flow complexity rather than field margin calls themselves.
thesis🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alphatrend followingliquiditymargin calls
So what: Alpha sleeves with weekly or monthly dealing terms are structurally unsuited to portable alpha regardless of their standalone Sharpe.
“They do not want to be getting margin calls um on a Monday. Please send cash by by end of day.” ▶ 50:21
Leverage as a portfolio-construction tool 4
A 50/50 equity-bond portfolio carries roughly 85% of its risk in equities and 15% in bonds, so the risk-balanced optimum is closer to 30% equities and 70% bonds, a portfolio nobody accepts unlevered because its return is too low.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: high60/40risk parityequitiesbondsSharpe ratio
So what: Leverage is what converts a higher-Sharpe risk-balanced allocation into an acceptable return level; refusing leverage means accepting an equity-dominated risk profile.
“85% of the risk is equities, 15% is bonds, even though on paper it says 50/50” ▶ 1:11:38
The ported alpha must not itself be more beta, because layering an additional directional equity-like driver on top of 100% beta increases volatility and enlarges the left tail rather than diversifying it.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highportable alphabetaalpha selection
So what: Screen portable alpha engines for hidden equity beta first; equity-like alpha in a levered stack is the fastest route to the structure failing.
“You almost certainly don't want to be introducing more beta, right? That's a risk in the same direction.” ▶ 1:12:47
The correct test for a ported alpha is not full-sample correlation but correlation in the worst 5% of beta returns, because liquidity-provision strategies like short volatility, merger arbitrage and the Treasury basis trade look excellent in normal times and break precisely in the shock.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highshort volatilitymerger arbitrageTreasury basis tradeportable alphacorrelation
So what: Run the conditional-correlation screen on the worst 5% of equity months before approving any alpha for a levered stack; it disqualifies most carry-like strategies.
“What you also need to really investigate is not just that it's uncorrelated, but that it's uncorrelated in the left tail.” ▶ 1:12:59
Trend is uniquely suited to a levered structure because volatility spikes at turning points automatically shrink trend positions, so trend demands less margin exactly when liquidity-provision strategies are getting margin calls.
causal🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: hightrend followingvolatility targetingmarginTreasury basis trade
So what: Vol-scaled position sizing gives trend a negative margin beta in crises, which is the mechanical reason portable alpha with trend survives shocks that kill other stacks.
“where people are worried about margin calls, you'd be especially worried about this in like a liquidity provision strategy like the basis trade or other other structures like that. Trend is actually reducing positions.” ▶ 1:14:48
Turtle rules as early decision theory 6
The turtles' N was a range-based volatility estimator from open, high, low and close with roughly a 14-day exponential decay half-life, later validated by Parkinson as an efficient way to estimate volatility.
data_point🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highTurtle tradersParkinson estimatorvolatility estimation
So what: Range-based volatility estimators remain a legitimate, low-data-cost alternative to close-to-close vol in systematic risk models.
“the turtles have a property n which is essentially the volatility of of of a market and they use a rangebased estimate estimator very simple to update each day. It's essentially like an exponential decay halfife of 14.”
The turtles' 'unit' concept made one unit of crude oil equivalent in risk terms to one unit of 10-year Treasuries even if the Treasury position required five times the dollar amount, which is volatility-based position sizing implemented in the 1980s.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highTurtle traderscrude oil10-year Treasuryvolatility targeting
So what: Risk-equalised rather than dollar-equalised sizing is the oldest and most durable feature of trend following, and its absence in a manager's process is a red flag.
“such that one unit of each commodity or financial asset they were trading would be equivalent in risk terms” ▶ 1:00:46
The turtles' 2N stop equated to risking no more than 2% of total equity per position, which the paper links to the Kelly criterion, and their sizing sat well to the left of the Kelly peak, sacrificing upside for a much lower probability of ruin.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highTurtle tradersKelly criterionEd Thorpruin theory
So what: Fractional Kelly is the practical standard: any manager sitting at or beyond full Kelly is optimising growth at the cost of survival probability.
“any prudent manager will be on the left side of that hill” ▶ 1:03:10
Unable to invert covariance matrices with pen and paper, the turtles handled correlation between markets through position-size caps on correlated markets rather than inside the portfolio construction.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highTurtle traderscorrelationportfolio constructioncovariance matrix
So what: Caps on correlated clusters are a robust, estimation-error-free substitute for covariance optimisation, useful when correlations are unstable.
“they weren't inverting covariance matrices um they had to have rules of thumb” ▶ 1:04:22
The turtles ran an explicit drawdown-control rule, cutting traded equity by about 20% for every 10% lost, which the paper connects to Grossman and Zhou's 1990s work on optimal drawdown control.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumTurtle tradersGrossman and Zhoudrawdown controlmulti-strategy pods
So what: Drawdown de-gearing exists because allocators and pension participants cannot tolerate an 80% drawdown even if the strategy would recover; it is a client constraint, not a maths result.
“I think they used a they lost 10% um in the market then they would cut their equity by 20%” ▶ 1:06:12
The host argues the turtles' genius was never the 100-day breakout entry but the risk approach, and that CTAs are risk managers first because they control the risk taken but not the returns generated.
thesis🗣 Niels Kaastrup-Larsen (host)conviction: highTurtle tradersCTAstrend followingrisk management
So what: Due diligence on trend managers should weight risk architecture over signal design, since signals are commoditised and risk control is where dispersion comes from.
“the genius was not so much in buying a 100 day breakout. The genius was as you point out in the way they approach risk.” ▶ 1:07:58
AI inside quant research and the junior hiring problem 4
Moore says the several hundred hours he spent learning Python and pandas at Man Group about seven years ago are now essentially a complete waste given current AI tools.
anecdote🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highMan GroupPythonAI coding toolsquant research
So what: Implementation skill in quant finance is depreciating fast, which changes both hiring criteria and the cost base of research-heavy managers.
“that was essentially a complete waste of time. So to the level that I can program it's absolutely complete useless waste of 200 hours um sort of seven years ago now” ▶ 30:49
The value case for AI in quant research is shifting researchers from roughly 50% creative thinking and 50% implementation to something closer to 90/10, because AI can summarise everything written before but cannot generate brand-new high-alpha quantitative ideas.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: highAIquant researchMan Groupproductivity
So what: The productivity gain is real but concentrated in commoditised work, so expect research throughput to rise without a proportional rise in genuine alpha discovery.
“if you could split that 50/50 and make it more like 9010” ▶ 32:04
Junior and graduate quant roles built around business-as-usual implementation are being squeezed out as processes that used to require humans get automated.
prediction🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumquant researchgraduate hiringAI automationMan Group
So what: Headcount growth at systematic managers should decouple from AUM growth, and the industry's talent pipeline faces a structural gap at the entry level.
“those very junior level graduate type roles where you would spend more time on the kind of BAU implementation etc. like it's kind of being squeezed out by by these tools” ▶ 32:40
Moore's proposed interview method is to hand a candidate an AI-generated piece of work and ask them to critique and improve it, because isolating candidates from AI tools no longer tests real working conditions.
framework🗣 Harry Mooreconviction: mediumhiringAIquant researchinterviews
So what: Critical evaluation of machine output is becoming the scarce human skill in research organisations, and firms that test for it will hire better.
“locking someone in a Wi-Fi free, computer free box is not a good test of of what working life looks like today”

0xResearch 1 episode(s) · 36 insights

Can Aerodrome Become Ethereum’s Dominant Spot Exchange? | Alexander Cutler66 min36 insights2026-08-11
▶ Watch on YouTube
Alex Cutler, CEO of Dromos and core contributor to Aerodrome and Velodrome, details the MetaDex03 upgrade that merges the two protocols into one exchange under a single token and expands to Ethereum mainnet and Circle's Arc, adding a 'rev engine' that internalizes MEV and cross-chain bridge fees plus an 'air engine' that ties emissions to pool productivity. His core thesis is that paying liquidity providers the most — by wrapping 100% of protocol revenue into the locked token — is the only durable moat in onchain spot markets, citing 40–60% of onchain FX volume and a claimed 70% flow collapse in Uniswap v4's USDC/USDT pool on Base after its fee switch. He sizes spot DEXs as the largest onchain fee vertical outside stablecoins at $5B last year and argues FX, RWAs and tokenized equities are the next growth verticals.
MetaDex03 upgrade and the Aerodrome–Velodrome merger · 5Chain selection: Base, Circle Arc, Ethereum mainnet · 6LP payout economics as the only moat · 7MEV internalization through dynamic fees · 3FX, RWAs and tokenized equities as the next spot verticals · 5AMMs versus prop AMMs and order books · 3Regulation, token disclosure and cycle sentiment · 7
MetaDex03 upgrade and the Aerodrome–Velodrome merger 5
Aerodrome and Velodrome are being merged into a single exchange united around a single token as part of the MetaDex03 upgrade, ending the two-protocol structure that has existed since Velodrome launched on Optimism roughly four years ago.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeVelodromeDromosMetaDex03OptimismBase
So what: A single-token merger changes value accrual for both AERO and VELO holders and concentrates all cross-chain revenue into one claim — the key structural change to model before the upgrade ships.
“not only will aerodrome and drrome be coming together into a single exchange united around a single token” ▶ 2:25
MetaDex03 adds a 'rev engine' that internalizes revenue streams beyond swap fees — specifically MEV capture and cross-chain bridge fees — and routes that value back to the token.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeMetaDex03MEVbridge feesAERO
So what: Expands the revenue base a locked-token holder claims on, so valuation multiples computed on swap fees alone will understate post-upgrade economics.
“we have the rev engine which is allowing us to internalize additional revenue streams like me capture u crosschain bridge fees right and direct that value right back to the token” ▶ 3:15
The 'air engine' makes AERO emissions dynamic and responsive, aligning the emissions rate directly to each pool's productivity in order to lower the overall rate at which the token emits.
framework🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeMetaDex03AEROemissionsliquidity providers
So what: Revenue-per-unit-of-emissions is the core valuation lever for ve(3,3) DEXs; productivity-linked emissions should improve that ratio and cut structural sell pressure.
“you can just think of it as more value is coming in and less value is coming out of our of our system” ▶ 3:52
Aerodrome claims it has generated more revenue than any other exchange over the past six months, both in absolute terms and relative to emissions paid out.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeDEX revenueemissions
So what: Revenue relative to emissions is the cleanest efficiency screen for incentive-driven DEXs; if verifiable it makes Aerodrome the benchmark for any competing spot venue.
“we have done more revenue in the last uh 6 months than any other uh exchange” ▶ 4:01
MetaSwaps launches as a combined cross-chain swapper, bridge and aggregator in one product that abstracts away bridging, which token is on which network, and even holding gas on the destination chain.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highMetaSwapsAerodromecross-chain bridgingDEX aggregators
So what: If it works, Aerodrome captures the bridge and aggregator fee layers that currently accrue to third parties — a direct competitive threat to those categories.
“the core of which is MetaSwaps which is the first combined crosschain swapper bridge and uh aggregator allin-one product” ▶ 4:39
Chain selection: Base, Circle Arc, Ethereum mainnet 6
Cutler argues block space is oversupplied and that generalized, undifferentiated L2s will consolidate, because copy-paste ecosystems give activity no reason to persist once liquidity incentives end.
thesis🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highEthereum L2sblock spaceDEXslending protocols
So what: Bearish for generic L2 tokens whose activity is incentive-funded; favors chains with a native distribution or asset-issuance edge.
“the issue was not lack of block space right the issue is what is that block space being used for” ▶ 7:39
The early bet on Base was driven by Coinbase's vertical integration turning the chain into a distribution vehicle — Coinbase.com's Bitcoin lending product is Morpho on Base under the hood, and any token launched on Aerodrome trades instantly on Coinbase.com.
causal🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highBaseCoinbaseMorphoAerodromeBitcoin
So what: The Coinbase distribution loop, not block space, is the moat for Base-native protocols — a screening criterion for which chains to deploy on next.
“today, any token that launches on a drone trades instantly on Coinbase.com, right? we have access to millions and millions of users” ▶ 9:17
Circle's Arc has a planned mainnet launch on September 16 with Aerodrome named as a day-one partner.

“yesterday circles arc announced plan mainet launch for September 16th with a drrome named as a day one partner” ▶ 6:32
FX is the specific vertical Aerodrome is targeting on Arc, because the binding constraint on scaling onchain FX has been the quality of the tokenized representations of the underlying currencies — something Circle is positioned to fix by working with issuers.
causal🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highCircleArcFXEURCUSDCAerodromeRWAs
So what: Wrapped-currency quality is the unlock for onchain FX volume; track the depth of new issuer-backed FX pairs on Arc as the leading indicator.
“the big issue in scaling FX onchain uh has been the quality of of uh the representations of the underlying assets” ▶ 11:14
Aerodrome already offers better execution on the EURC/USDC pair than Wise, and far better than a bank charging multiple percentage points to convert between the two currencies.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeEURCUSDCWiseFXpayments
So what: A concrete cost benchmark showing onchain FX can undercut fintech remittance rails — the wedge for payment-rail integrations.
“we can give better execution today on Aerad Drrome than you can get on Wise” ▶ 11:35
The Ethereum mainnet playbook is explicitly modeled on the SushiSwap vampire attack, where simply offering LPs a premium over Uniswap drained 50% of Uniswap's TVL within one week; Cutler notes neither Velodrome nor Aerodrome was first in its ecosystem yet both reached number one.
framework🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highSushiSwapUniswapAerodromeVelodromeEthereum mainnetTVLCurve
So what: Gives a historical magnitude for how fast mercenary TVL can move if mainnet Aerodrome rewards clear Uniswap's — watch mainnet TVL migration in the weeks after launch.

LP payout economics as the only moat 7
Cutler reduces DEX competition to a single variable: liquidity providers are a highly mercenary group who will move to whichever venue pays the best reward for the same service, so the highest-paying venue takes share mechanically.
framework🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeliquidity providersDEXs
So what: Reduces DEX due diligence to a measurable LP-yield spread across venues rather than a narrative about technology.
“if you're a liquidity provider and a venue is offering you a dollar and another venue is offering you a buck 50 where are you going to park your liquidity right” ▶ 17:04
Aerodrome can pay LPs more than swap fees alone justify because AERO wraps the exchange's full current and future value — swap fees plus liquidity incentives plus launch payments — so emitting AERO hands LPs a claim on that entire bundle.
framework🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAEROAerodromeveAEROincentivestoken launches
So what: Explains why AERO can sustain a premium to pure swap-fee multiples, and why token price and LP acquisition cost are the same lever.
“that collective value isn't just swap fees. Um it's liquidity payments uh or incentives. It's launch payments and and incentives. So it represents a justified sort of premium over swap fees alone.” ▶ 16:52
Locked AERO earns 100% of the exchange's revenue at any given time, with supply limits enforced by immutable code and emissions controlled entirely by voters onchain.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAEROveAEROAerodromegovernance
So what: Full pass-through with no protocol treasury skim is the differentiator against fee-switch models — the basis for direct yield comparison against UNI.
“you can take Arrow uh lock it and earn 100% of the exchanges revenue um at any given time” ▶ 16:37
Uniswap's fee switch redirects up to 25% of the fees a liquidity provider earns across v2 and v3 back to UNI, which Cutler frames as a structural handicap because Aerodrome only has to beat 75 cents on the dollar.
causal🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highUniswapUNIfee switchAerodromeliquidity providers
So what: The fee switch is a share-loss risk for Uniswap, not just incremental revenue; favors venues that still pass LPs the full dollar.
“it's like up to 25% of the fees a liquidity provider is is earning might be redirected back to uh Uniurn. So, uh we can certainly offer you more than 75 cents on the dollar.” ▶ 19:54
Cutler cites data showing that after Uniswap v4's fee was applied to the USDC/USDT pool on Base, flows through that pool fell roughly 70% — and fees with them — with the displaced flow migrating to Aerodrome.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highUniswap v4USDCUSDTBaseAerodromefee switch
So what: A live empirical case that DEX fee extraction is highly demand-elastic — a warning for revenue models that assume flows are sticky after a fee switch.
“this tax that they have put on the flows that are coming in resulted in like a 70% drop off in the amount of flows flowing through that pool which is of course 70% drop off in the fees. And where did those flows go? Right over to Aerodrome.” ▶ 20:23
Hyperliquid is presented as the second proof point of the maximum-distribution thesis, distributing roughly 97% of the value the exchange captures back to the token and, like Aerodrome, having forgone VC funding, which took it from one of many perp venues to the definitive one in its category.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highHyperliquidHYPEGMXdYdXventure capital
So what: Sets a distribution-ratio benchmark near 97–100% that competing token models must approach to be defensible.
“they distribute I think 97% or so of all the value captured um by the exchange back to the token” ▶ 22:44
Aerodrome does buyback-and-lock rather than buyback-and-burn, on the logic that the token is a claim on revenues passed through in the form they accrue, so the protocol funds its own growth from the same revenue stream every other holder receives.
framework🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highAerodromeAEROveAERObuybackstoken burns
So what: A distinct tokenomic design to contrast against burn-based models when assessing which mechanism actually compounds holder claims while still funding development.
“we're not buying back and burning the token. Um, or at least that's not the the primary mechanism of value redistribution. We do buybacks and locking”
MEV internalization through dynamic fees 3
Cutler sizes MEV as hundreds of millions of dollars spent and captured across chains, making it a large addressable revenue pool for an AMM able to internalize part of it.
data_point🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: mediumMEVsequencersAMMsAerodrome
So what: Frames the upside of the rev engine: even a slice of MEV is a material new revenue line relative to current DEX fee bases.
“hundreds of millions of dollars are are spent and captured um around [snorts] ME across uh different different chains” ▶ 28:33
Aerodrome's MEV auction works by having its dynamic fee module detect the chain-level priority fee auction and raise pool fees dynamically and proportionally to that onchain signal, redirecting part of the arbitrage value from the sequencer to liquidity providers.
framework🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highMEVSlipstreamdynamic feessequencerssearchersParadigm
So what: A replicable mechanism design: any AMM that can read the priority-fee signal can claw MEV back from sequencers, which is a revenue threat to L2 sequencer economics.
“what you can do with that dynamic fee module is it can detect um at the chain level that priority fee auction um on the underlying chain” ▶ 31:10
Cutler expects the MEV auction to feel roughly neutral to searchers rather than disruptive: on a $100 arbitrage where a searcher bids $10, total spend stays near $10 but roughly 20% now goes to the AMM instead of entirely to the sequencer.
prediction🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: mediumMEVsearcherssequencersAMMsarbitrage
So what: Implies the cost lands on sequencer/L2 revenue rather than on searcher participation — expect little flow loss but a hit to chain-level MEV take rates.
“they'll still probably pay in uh something like $10, but maybe now 20% of it is going over to the AMM and and the rest is going to the sequencer” ▶ 33:31
FX, RWAs and tokenized equities as the next spot verticals 5
Cutler argues low crypto-native trading volumes are an asset-supply problem rather than a demand problem — years of low-float high-FDV and extractive issuance mean there simply are not many good tokens worth trading.
contrarian🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highaltcoinslow float high FDVtrading volumedigital assets

As of last year's check roughly 90% of the top 100 tokens redistributed no value at all, and Cutler predicts increasing consolidation into the minority that do, as already happened around HYPE.
prediction🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: hightop 100 tokensHyperliquidHYPEvalue accrual
So what: A concrete allocation filter: concentrate in the ~10% of tokens with real distribution and expect the rest to keep losing relative share.

Onchain equities and currency trading is counter-cyclical to crypto's four-year cycle, so routing that flow through spot exchanges creates fee revenue that persists through crypto drawdowns.

Aerodrome is the top onchain venue most weeks with 40–60% of onchain FX volume, and in many weeks this year has done more FX volume than every other decentralized exchange on every other chain combined.

AMMs versus prop AMMs and order books 3
Cutler concedes prop AMMs have found traction on Solana around a handful of blue-chip assets but argues their fatal flaw is that all liquidity on those platforms disappears exactly during black-swan events, permanently capping their share of onchain markets that depend on reliable pricing.
contrarian🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highprop AMMsSolanaAMMsmarket makersBinance
So what: A structural argument for why lending protocols and oracles cannot depend on prop-AMM liquidity, capping the category's role in composable DeFi.
“all the liquidity on those platforms disappears the minute it is absolutely needed” ▶ 41:56
Cutler flags unresolved spoofing in prop AMM books — aggregators and large takers cannot fully trust the quotes, some reported volume may reflect misquoted flow, and he predicts regulators will scrutinize the practice once the category stops being niche.
prediction🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: mediumprop AMMsspoofingaggregatorsregulatorsmarket structure
So what: Treat prop-AMM volume statistics as potentially overstated, and treat scale as a regulatory trigger for the category rather than validation of it.
“if you know anything about spoofing in traditional markets the moment they are not niche uh that is going to be something that I think regulators are going to take a very very close look at” ▶ 43:13
An operator of one of the largest prop AMMs across several networks told Cutler that Base is the ecosystem where they have struggled most to find traction, because Aerodrome rewards liquidity providers at a rate that makes competing for that flow uneconomical.
anecdote🗣 Alexander Cutlerconviction: highprop AMMsBaseAerodromeliquidity providers
So what: First-hand evidence that LP-payout intensity, not technology, is what repels professional market makers — a pattern to test on other chains.
“Bass is actually the ecosystem where they have struggled most to find any traction” ▶ 45:18

Nikhil Kamath 1 episode(s) · 35 insights

The Battery Everyone Uses Is The One They'd Never Build | EnerVenue x HiNa | WTF are Batteries?70 min35 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Nikhil Kamath interviews Henning, CEO of EnerVenue (a Stanford/NASA-derived nickel-hydrogen cell company building a gigafactory in Changzhou), and Kun Tang, CEO of a Chinese sodium-ion battery maker spun out of Prof. Liquan Chen's lab, on why LFP's cost dominance carries an unpriced fire risk — over 14,000 LFP fires globally in 2025 — and why the next leg of storage growth is non-lithium, stationary and localised. Both argue India should skip EV-brand manufacturing and build sodium-ion or nickel-hydrogen cells for grid and AI data-centre storage at roughly $15M per GWh of capex, deliberately decoupled from Chinese lithium and rare-earth supply chains.
LFP dominance and the unpriced fire risk · 5Sodium-ion: cheap inputs, hard density ceiling · 5Nickel-hydrogen as the safe stationary bet · 5Supply-chain decoupling and the India entry case · 5China industrial policy and the EV shakeout · 5Data centres, diesel displacement and decentralised energy · 4Generation mix, super cycles and AI power demand · 6
LFP dominance and the unpriced fire risk 5
The world recorded over 14,000 LFP battery fires in 2025, a rate far above the 'once in a while' impression created by occasional news coverage of Tesla or BYD fires.
data_point🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highLFPlithium iron phosphateTeslaBYDEnerVenue
So what: A fire base rate this large is the commercial opening for non-flammable chemistries in stationary and data-centre storage; watch insurers and data-centre operators repricing LFP risk.
“Last year, 2025, the world had over 14,000 LFP fires. It is not once in a while.” ▶ 1:14
Battery safety risk scales with energy density, so the industry's push for longer range structurally increases fire risk rather than reducing it, and thermal runaway becomes self-accelerating past a tipping temperature.
framework🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highLFPenergy densitythermal runawaysolid-state batteries
So what: Any energy-density roadmap needs a matching safety-cost assumption; claims of an intrinsically safe high-density cell deserve skepticism.
“frankly speaking, the higher energy, the more safety issues because the energy-- Just like the pump is so high but the tube here is so thin that it's easy to break.” ▶ 20:31
LFP cell safety depends as much on manufacturing quality as on chemistry: tier-2 Chinese producers show materially higher safety-incident rates, which is why CATL sustainably prices about 10% above tier-2 cells.
data_point🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highCATLBYDLFPtier-2 Chinese cell makers
So what: A durable ~10% quality premium means buying cells on price alone is a hidden liability trade, and margin-compression theses on CATL should account for this safety-driven pricing power.
“So, tier 2 Chinese companies, the ratio of their safety issues, always much higher than top BYD, like CATL. So that's why CATL always sell about 10% expensive than the tier two.” ▶ 22:53
Large-scale stationary LFP storage bought today delivers roughly 10,000 cycles before augmentation, ending at about 60% residual capacity along a degradation curve.
data_point🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highLFPstationary storagegrid storage
So what: 10,000 cycles to 60% is the benchmark any competing stationary chemistry must beat on levelised cost, and it sets the augmentation capex schedule in storage project models.
“if you buy a large scale stationary storage right now, you will get roughly 10,000 cycles out of this” ▶ 23:48
Cell-to-pack packaging let cheap LFP reach roughly 1,000 km of vehicle range, which is why the industry migrated away from higher-density NMC despite its nickel/cobalt/manganese energy advantage.
causal🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highLFPNMCcell-to-packnickelcobaltmanganese
So what: Pack-level engineering, not cathode chemistry, delivered the last range step — bearish for cobalt/nickel cathode demand from passenger EVs and for density-only startup pitches.
“we have new technology use, we call it a C2P, cell to pack-- So the LFP can-- You can drive LFP driven car to up to 1000 kilometres long range” ▶ 19:21
Sodium-ion: cheap inputs, hard density ceiling 5
Sodium carbonate costs roughly one-tenth of lithium carbonate and its price is flat rather than volatile, making sodium-ion's raw-material input both cheaper and far more predictable than lithium's.
data_point🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highsodium carbonatelithium carbonatesodium-ion battery
So what: Price stability, not just level, is the sodium pitch — it de-risks long-duration storage offtake contracts that the lithium carbonate cycle has repeatedly blown up.
“So the price of cobalt, sodium carbonate is one-tenth of the lithium carbonate. Okay, low cost. It's flat, no variation.” ▶ 40:04
Sodium-ion cells sit at roughly 110-120 energy-density units versus LFP, and every attempt to push density toward LFP levels has reintroduced the safety failures that sodium was chosen to avoid.
data_point🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highsodium-ion batteryLFPenergy density
So what: Sodium's safety edge is conditional on staying low-density, so sodium is a grid and short-range story; any 'sodium matches LFP' claim should be stress-tested on penetration test results.
“During the improvement, we increase the energy density close to LFP, there some safety issue comes up.” ▶ 40:51
Sodium-ion is viable for three-wheelers, scooters and sub-500 km vehicles but not for 1,000 km long-range cars, which remain lithium or eventual solid-state territory.
thesis🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highsodium-ion batteryscootersthree-wheelerssolid-state batteries
So what: Maps sodium demand directly onto India's two- and three-wheeler fleet plus stationary storage — a segment an investor can size, unlike generic 'sodium replaces lithium' claims.
“We can use for the three-wheels, scooters, short range, which is less than 500 kilometres, it's fine.” ▶ 41:36
Sodium-ion uses essentially the same manufacturing equipment as lithium cells with only the active materials swapped, which is why most incumbent lithium producers already claim a sodium product line.
causal🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highsodium-ion batterylithium-iongigafactory equipment
So what: Low switching cost means sodium has almost no equipment moat against incumbents — the defensible IP is cathode and anode materials, and CATL-scale players can enter fast.
“We are at the same level with lithium. So we use actually, the similar facility with lithium. So we just change the material. So that's why most of the lithium battery company, they claim they have sodium product.” ▶ 43:23
India cannot beat Chinese lithium-ion on cost once exports flow, but can compete in sodium-ion because the chemistry is new, its inputs (sodium, iron, phosphate) are unconstrained commodity chemicals, and India's scooter fleet plus grid storage supply the demand.
thesis🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: highIndiasodium-ion batterylithium-ionChinasodiumironphosphate
So what: The specific entry argument for Indian cell manufacturing: pick the chemistry where China's scale advantage has not yet compounded, not the one where it has.
“If you beat-- Choose lithium-ion battery, once there's export from China, no one can beat the whole cost. But for sodium battery, it's more localised that you can get your own sodium resources.” ▶ 58:21
Nickel-hydrogen as the safe stationary bet 5
EnerVenue's Aqueous Metal Cell is a NASA-derived nickel-hydrogen technology incubated at Stanford, built only from nickel, steel, a water-based electrolyte and glass fibre — no lithium, cobalt or rare earths in the bill of materials.
data_point🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highEnerVenuenickel-hydrogenAqueous Metal CellNASAStanford Universitynickel
So what: A storage chemistry whose entire input list is nickel plus commodity materials is the cleanest expression of the decouple-from-Chinese-critical-minerals trade.
“we use nickel, we use steel, we use water-based electrolyte and a little bit of glass fibre. That's it, what you need in order to create this battery.” ▶ 35:22
The nickel-hydrogen cell claims 30,000 cycles, minimal efficiency loss from -10C to +45C, and no thermal-runaway pathway because the water-based electrolyte removes the fire trigger — at the cost of energy density too low for any mobile application.
data_point🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highEnerVenuenickel-hydrogenthermal runawaystationary storagedata centres
So what: 30,000 cycles against LFP's ~10,000 is a 3x levelised-cost lever for daily-cycling grid assets, while the deliberate concession of mobility narrows the market to containers, grid and data centres.
“We can run 30,000 cycles compared to all other chemistries all competing by a couple of X.” ▶ 36:47
EnerVenue has raised over $300 million and is building its first gigafactory in Changzhou, China, scaling past one gigawatt next year, while remaining a US-domiciled Silicon Valley company.
data_point🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highEnerVenueChangzhouChinaSilicon Valley
So what: A US company deliberately manufacturing in China is a live test of whether cross-border 'best of both worlds' structures survive the decoupling its own CEO forecasts.
“With EnerVenue, we just raised $300 plus million and we're building a first gigafactory as we speak in Changzhou.” ▶ 37:44
EnerVenue concedes it has not yet reached the cost curve versus scaled lithium and is banking on gigawatt-scale volume next year to become price competitive.
thesis🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: mediumEnerVenuenickel-hydrogenlithiumcost curve
So what: Candid admission that today's pitch is safety and cycle life, not price — the investable question is whether one GW of volume closes a gap LFP spent a decade widening.
“So we are not hitting the cost curve yet, but next year we are scaling over a gigawatt, and I think then we will be very competitive.” ▶ 37:52
EnerVenue intends never to sell the nickel: customers buy the battery but rent the nickel through a special-purpose vehicle, with up to 98% reclaimed after the 30-year life and sold back to the original supplier, whose processing sits in China with mining in Indonesia.
framework🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highEnerVenuenickelspecial purpose vehiclerecyclingIndonesiaChina
So what: Metal-as-a-service converts commodity price exposure into a financeable residual asset, but the loop still terminates in Chinese refining — the thing to localise in India is processing, not mining.
“You buy the battery from us, but you rent the nickel from us. And after the lifetime, we are taking back the batteries.” ▶ 44:50
Supply-chain decoupling and the India entry case 5
Lithium and rare earths account for roughly 10% or less of a lithium cell's wholesale cost, so resource nationalism is primarily a supply-availability risk rather than a cost risk.
data_point🗣 Henning and Kun Tangconviction: mediumlithiumrare earthsbattery cell costs
So what: Reframes the India rare-earth mining case: capturing 10% of cell BOM domestically does not fix cell competitiveness — the value sits in refining, cathode and manufacturing scale.
“Lithium cost, I think, 10%. Rare earth is 10% of the wholesale? Wholesale, yes. Less than 10%.” ▶ 34:30
The explicit advice to a new entrant is to avoid rare earths and lithium entirely and pick a chemistry supplyable from local resources, because geopolitics will not improve for the next 10 to 15 years.
thesis🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highrare earthslithiumIndiageopoliticssupply chain
So what: The clearest actionable filter in the episode: screen battery ventures on whether the bill of materials survives an export ban, not on cost per kWh today.
“Don't start a battery company where you need rare earth. Decouple your supply chain from lithium and rare earth. Create a new battery technology that you can actually supply with your local markets available.” ▶ 35:07
Lithium is geographically concentrated and, per CATL's Robin Zeng speaking at the same forum, some producer countries are moving to ban lithium exports, while lithium refining remains overwhelmingly concentrated in China.
causal🗣 Kun Tang, sodium-ion battery CEOconviction: mediumlithiumCATLRobin ZengChinalithium refining
So what: Two stacked chokepoints — resource nationalism upstream and Chinese refining midstream — mean lithium supply risk cannot be diversified by opening new mines alone.
“there are some countries that will say that this is-- Were not allowed to export anymore.” ▶ 39:23
Both CEOs put the capex to reach a saleable battery product at roughly $50 million, with cell capacity costing about 100 million RMB (~$15 million) per gigawatt-hour, and say it can be done in India without government subsidies given product-market fit.
data_point🗣 Henning and Kun Tangconviction: highIndiagigafactorycapexsodium-ion batterynickel-hydrogen
So what: A concrete unit-economics anchor — ~$15M/GWh — that makes cell manufacturing cheap relative to AI-infrastructure capex and shifts diligence from policy dependence to offtake.
“You know, lithium, the claim is 100 million RMB per gigawatt hour. Yes, similar. So let's say $15 million per gigawatt hour.” ▶ 43:40
Asked directly whether to start an EV company or a battery company in India, the EnerVenue CEO said he would not start a car company and would build batteries instead, because what India actually needs is energy independence.
thesis🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highIndiaelectric vehiclesbattery manufacturingenergy independence
So what: From an operator who watched China's shakeout: the durable Indian opportunity is cells and storage, not another EV brand chasing a commoditised, contract-manufactured product.
“would I start a car company? No, I wouldn't. I would start a battery company because what India needs is certain, let's call it energy independence.” ▶ 33:56
China industrial policy and the EV shakeout 5
China's government functioned as a state venture capitalist across batteries, EVs and PV modules — funding roughly 20 companies per target sector expecting five to survive as world leaders, sometimes retaining equity — inside five-year plans nested in 20-30 year visions.
framework🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highChinaindustrial policybatterieselectric vehiclesPV modules
So what: The reusable model for reading Chinese sector entry: assume deliberate overfunding then brutal consolidation, so early market-share data means little until the shakeout finishes.
“They are throwing a bunch of money in the ring and saying, guys, I'm funding 20 companies right now. Five will survive and they will be world leading.” ▶ 33:35
China's EV sector consolidated from roughly 400 brands to an expected 10-12 survivors over a 20-year subsidy war that included widespread subsidy fraud, leaving BYD and CATL as the two giants.
data_point🗣 Kun Tang and Henningconviction: highBYDCATLChinaelectric vehiclessubsidies
So what: Sets the realistic survivor ratio for any subsidised EV or cell buildout, including India's incentive-backed entrants, where the same math implies most funded players are write-offs.
“finally, there were ten on the table. 10, 12 will survive.” ▶ 29:59
German carmakers lost the EV race on software and system integration rather than mechanical engineering, because Chinese entrants skipped ICE expertise and built controller-, software- and drivetrain-integrated products from the start.
contrarian🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highGermanyBYDLixiangChinaelectric vehicles
So what: The moat in EVs is software and system integration, not cells or assembly — a screen for which legacy OEMs can plausibly catch up.
“the Germans, I think, innovated on cars too slow. They were captured in their own think tank and they were all competed by China.” ▶ 27:39
China's 'tier-zero supplier' ecosystem lets anyone commission a fully manufactured own-brand car with essentially zero vertical integration, collapsing the barrier to entry for car brands while destroying their differentiation.
framework🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highChinatier-zero supplierselectric vehiclescontract manufacturing
So what: If a car brand can be ordered like a private-label product, brand-only EV startups have no defensible margin and value accrues to the tier-zero integrators and cell makers.
“You can basically order a "Nikhil-type-one" car and then you can design it as you like, and you get it manufactured and then you have your own car company. But how vertically integrated are you? Basically zero. You just create a brand.” ▶ 27:53
BYD is no longer a car company but a battery, semiconductor and renewable-energy conglomerate that integrated upstream into mining and downstream into vehicles and platform revenue, mirroring Xiaomi's ecosystem playbook.
thesis🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highBYDXiaomisemiconductorsminingbatteries
So what: Valuing BYD on auto multiples alone misses the vertically integrated component and energy businesses that fund its price war.
“if you go to BYD's headquarters today, BYD is not only a car company. It is a battery, it's a semiconductor company, it is a renewable energy company.” ▶ 31:31
Data centres, diesel displacement and decentralised energy 4
Data-centre operators are actively hesitant to deploy LFP inside facilities, giving safer chemistries strong traction in UPS and primary power because a battery premium is trivial against a billion-dollar burn-down risk.
thesis🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highdata centresLFPUPSEnerVenuehyperscalers
So what: The most immediately investable demand channel here: AI data-centre storage procurement is a safety-first, relatively price-insensitive buyer — a premium-margin niche for non-flammable cells.
“if you spend a little bit more on a battery, but you have full safety, it is well invested money to not burn down your data centre for billion dollars.” ▶ 47:05
The residential virtual-power-plant model proven at Enpal — solar, EV chargers, batteries, heat pumps and smart metering arbitraging cheap and expensive grid hours — reaches 80-90% household energy autarky in Europe.
data_point🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenue and former Enpal operatorconviction: highEnpalEuropevirtual power plantresidential solarheat pumps
So what: 80-90% autarky is the benchmark for VPP-style residential energy businesses, and by the guests' own pricing logic it works in 30-cent Germany but not yet in 6-cent India.
“we would run what we call a virtual power plant. We arbitrage trade energy, basically, we are stabilising the grids.” ▶ 56:42
India's power price of roughly 5-6 rupees (about 6 cents) per unit is a fifth of Germany's 30 cents, which is why residential solar-plus-storage payback economics that work in Europe do not work in India.
causal🗣 Henning and Nikhil Kamathconviction: highIndiaGermanyresidential solarelectricity prices
So what: Cheap grid power is the structural headwind for Indian behind-the-meter storage — viable Indian storage demand is utility-scale and data-centre, not rooftop arbitrage.
“You have a six cent electricity bill. Germany has a 30 cent electricity bill.” ▶ 55:52
Cars sit idle roughly 98% of the time, so a plugged-in EV could simultaneously stabilise the grid and run its idle compute chips as a node in a decentralised data centre, extending the shift from centralised to distributed energy.
framework🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: mediumelectric vehiclesTeslavehicle-to-griddecentralised computingdata centres
So what: A speculative convergence thesis echoing Tesla's 'every car is a token factory', pointing at value in V2G software and distributed-compute orchestration rather than hardware.
“So why not having your car as a micro data centre in front of your home, solving computational issues and being part of a decentralised data centre” ▶ 54:42
Generation mix, super cycles and AI power demand 6
Both guests dismiss nuclear as a near-term bet — fusion is roughly 50 years away and small modular reactors cost about five times per unit of power — and argue the majority of humanity's energy must come from solar and wind with batteries, over a gas/hydro/nuclear base load.
contrarian🗣 Henning and Kun Tangconviction: highnuclearSMRsfusionsolarwindbatteries
So what: A direct rebuttal to the AI-driven nuclear and SMR trade from operators betting that solar plus wind plus storage is deployable now and cheaper.
“Even SMRs are very, very far away, right? Yeah. Like the small nuclear reactors, super cost. It costs five times the unit of power, so who's going to subsidise it?” ▶ 49:53
Solid-state batteries sit at roughly technology-readiness level four of nine per CATL's Robin Zeng, and despite constant press claims nobody has cracked commercial production.
data_point🗣 Kun Tang, quoting Robin Zeng of CATLconviction: highsolid-state batteriesCATLRobin Zengtechnology readiness
So what: A hard readiness number from the largest cell maker to discount solid-state announcements — it implies years before solid-state threatens LFP or sodium economics.
“he thinks that the solid-state battery currently at level four” ▶ 42:30
The investable macro is three overlapping super cycles — electrification and energy, manufacturing capability, and AI — all with demand exceeding supply, and the biggest opportunities sit where an entrepreneur combines all three.
framework🗣 Henning, CEO of EnerVenueconviction: highelectrificationmanufacturingAIenergy
So what: A clean allocation framework — energy, automated manufacturing capability, compute — with the edge going to businesses at the intersection rather than in a single silo.
“Let's imagine for a second you have abundant energy, you have abundant manufacturing capacity, and you have abundant computational power, you can do basically everything.” ▶ 1:01:21
China's much-discussed overcapacity is in finished products, not manufacturing capability — the world is short of automated manufacturing capacity and manufacturing input goods — while Chinese solar-cell overcapacity is already being exported globally, which is why India banned Chinese cell imports.
contrarian🗣 Henning and Kun Tangconviction: highChinaovercapacitymanufacturingsolar cellsIndiarobots
So what: Separates the deflationary product glut from a genuine capital-goods shortage — bullish for automation and manufacturing-equipment suppliers even amid Chinese price wars, and explains India's import-substitution rules.
“You have overcapacities in certain products, but you have undercapacity in manufacturing capabilities. That's a different thing, right?” ▶ 1:00:58
AI is a self-accelerating energy flywheel — more compute drives more power demand, cited as roughly 1,000 additional terawatt-hours by 2030 — which is pushing cell makers to reposition from vehicle customers toward AI data centres.
prediction🗣 Kun Tang and Henningconviction: highAIdata centrespower demandbatteriessolar
So what: Quantifies why battery makers are re-aiming at data-centre demand; the sodium CEO frames it as selling to the silicon-based market rather than the carbon-based one.
“we think that we want to sell our batteries, not only to carbon-based belts. We want to sell to the silicon-based market.” ▶ 1:03:22
The economic crossover where electrified renewables outcompete fossil fuels already happened in 2023, making green the cheapest option rather than an ideological choice, and no single chemistry will win the storage market.
thesis🗣 Henning and Kun Tangconviction: highrenewablesfossil fuelssolarsodium-ion batteryLFPnickel-hydrogen
So what: If the cost crossover is behind us, transition capital stops depending on carbon policy and starts depending on cost of capital and deployment speed — and storage exposure is best held as a basket of application-specific chemistry leaders.
“Going green is not some esoteric ideation anymore. It is the most economical way.” ▶ 57:21

Odd Lots · Bloomberg 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

NYT CEO Meredith Kopit Levien on Running a Media Brand in the Age of AI | Odd Lots61 min32 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
New York Times CEO Meredith Kopit Levien walks Odd Lots through the economics of the NYT bundle (entry at roughly $1/week with engineered price step-ups, a 'get everybody under the demand curve' strategy), the company's two-track AI posture (a licensing deal with Amazon alongside active suits against OpenAI, Microsoft and Perplexity, backed by ~$2B of annual content spend and a 175-year corpus), and a costly push into video that has coincided with post-earnings stock weakness. She insists news journalism — not games or sports — is the most economically value-creating product, and that family control lets the company fund long-horizon bets while editors, not finance, decide coverage spend.
Bundle pricing and the demand curve · 4AI licensing, litigation, and publisher leverage · 4AI inside the newsroom · 5Talent flywheel versus independent media · 4Video push and its ROI question · 5Capital allocation, family control, and M&A discipline · 5Audience capture, trust, and newsroom control · 5
Bundle pricing and the demand curve 4
The New York Times prices to capture the entire demand curve — from discounted international games-only subscriptions at the low end up to shared family-plan bundles at the top — rather than optimizing a single price point.
framework🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesNYT Games
So what: Segmented pricing plus product-led value increases is the mechanism behind NYT ARPU growth; watch ARPU and bundle mix rather than raw subscriber adds.
“our pricing strategy is meant to get everybody under the demand curve” ▶ 47:20
Most New York Times subscribers enter the full news-and-lifestyle bundle at about a dollar a week, engage over time, and are then moved to a materially higher price at a designed 'pricing step-up point,' a sequence the CEO says has gone 'really, really well.'
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesNYT bundle
So what: The promo-to-full-price conversion ladder, not headline subscriber counts, is the real driver of revenue per user; deceleration in step-up conversion would be the first crack in the model.
“most people buy our news title bundle or which is the whole thing at a dollar a week. And then over time they engage. And then over time they reach a pricing step up point and they come to a much higher price” ▶ 48:25
The Times refused the standard paywall trade-off, deliberately keeping large volumes of high-quality work free to sample while preserving enough 'gravity' behind the paywall — a calibration Levien says has been managed continuously for over a decade to seed 'the next million subscribers.'
framework🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York Times
So what: Explains why NYT paywall tightness oscillates; a hard paywall pivot would signal the top-of-funnel is broken.
“we never accepted that we had to choose between having a lot of stuff widely available so that people could sample our work, and also enough value and gravity in the product so that people would pay” ▶ 45:53
Levien frames a decade-long strategic priority of pulling audience relationships off third-party platforms and into direct NYT control, calling that migration part of why the Times succeeded.
causal🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesFacebookGoogle
So what: Direct-relationship ownership is what insulates NYT from AI-era search and social referral collapse that is hitting referral-dependent publishers.
“we've been keenly aware for the better part of a decade that the direction of travel from the platforms will be the we should be getting more and more of it into our own control” ▶ 46:41
AI licensing, litigation, and publisher leverage 4
The New York Times is running a dual-track AI strategy: it has signed a licensing deal with Amazon while simultaneously litigating against OpenAI and Microsoft (roughly three years in) and Perplexity (sued last December).
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highAmazonOpenAIMicrosoftPerplexityThe New York Times
So what: NYT is deliberately setting a litigation floor to raise the price of the deals it does sign — a template for content-owner leverage and a live legal overhang for OpenAI, Microsoft and Perplexity.
“We've done a deal. We have also, sued three companies were suing. It were two and a half years, maybe almost three now in to suing OpenAI and Microsoft. Three. We sued Perplexity last December.” ▶ 36:16
Levien sets three explicit conditions for any AI licensing deal: it must be continuous with a sustainable, growing publisher business; it must give the publisher explicit permission-granting and control over all uses; and it must deliver sustainable, fair value exchange.
framework🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesOpenAIAmazonLLMs
So what: A reusable term-sheet checklist for evaluating any publisher-AI deal; deals lacking use-level control are the ones NYT will litigate rather than sign.
The New York Times spent close to $2 billion last year producing roughly half a million works of content and has been producing at some scale for 175 years, a cost base Levien uses to argue the corpus is far more valuable than what AI companies currently pay for 'data.'
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesLLMs
So what: The hardest public number in the episode: ~$4,000 of production cost per work, a benchmark for pricing content licensing against AI-lab training budgets.
“we spent like close to $2 billion last year producing half a million works of content, stories, photos, videos, expressive, creative work that required enormous resource to go make and human beings to go make” ▶ 35:46
Levien argues that AI labs spending tens to hundreds of billions on talent, compute and power are underpaying for the journalism and creative work they call 'data,' framing content compensation as structurally mispriced relative to the rest of the AI capex stack.
contrarian🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highOpenAIMicrosoftPerplexityLLMsThe New York Times
So what: If content licensing repriced to even a small fraction of compute budgets, it becomes a material new revenue line for premium publishers and a new cost line for AI labs.
“These companies, the LLMs are spending, you know, tens of billions of dollars, sometimes hundreds of billions of dollars on talent, on compute, on power. They should be spending the money that is sufficient value for exchange.” ▶ 37:41
AI inside the newsroom 5
The New York Times has a dedicated AI initiatives team in the newsroom that built a tool set to cluster and pattern-match the roughly 3 million pages of Epstein files released on a Friday night, enabling stories the following week that would otherwise have taken far longer.
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesEpstein filesAI
So what: Concrete proof that AI's near-term publisher ROI is document-scale investigative speed, not text generation — a capability advantage that compounds for large newsrooms over small ones.
“The Epstein files dropped on a Friday night, and I think it was, like three, 3 million pages of documents.” ▶ 40:40
The Times used AI to disprove the social-media consensus on the Sydney Sweeney jeans controversy, showing the outrage was first constructed by the right rather than by the left — an example of AI enabling stories that could not previously be reported.
anecdote🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesSydney SweeneyAIsocial media
So what: AI-powered narrative-provenance analysis is a defensible new journalism product category that independent one-person outlets cannot easily replicate.
“the idea on social media was that it was like people on the left were outraged, the libs are outraged, and we actually used AI to prove that it was first a construction of the. Right.” ▶ 0:08
Levien draws a firm line that high-quality independent journalism and creative work must remain human-led, while accepting AI for efficiency, accessibility, automated voice and translation — where she sees real opportunity as the technology improves.
thesis🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesAItranslation
So what: AI translation and automated voice are the most likely near-term international TAM expansion levers for NYT without proportional editorial cost.
“the way we think about it today first is what's the work that should be human led? And I want to say high quality, independent journalism, human led, human led, creative stuff” ▶ 39:13
The Times employs close to a thousand people in digital product development and about 3,000 journalists and content makers, and Levien expects the journalism headcount to keep growing as long as the company keeps succeeding — an explicitly anti-AI-displacement view for creative roles.
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesAI
So what: Rebuts the thesis that AI compresses publisher opex; NYT margin expansion is expected from revenue growth, not editorial headcount cuts.
“we've got close to a thousand people doing digital product development in the New York Times, you know, 3000 journalists and people making content” ▶ 39:56
Levien says token spend has become the standard question among CEOs — she is on another public company board and 'everyone is talking about this' — but argues nobody can credibly forecast it yet given evaluation and human-judgment overhead, so NYT has not turned it into a multi-year capital planning line.
contrarian🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: mediumThe New York TimesClaude CodeCodexAI
So what: Useful check on AI-inference demand models: large enterprise buyers are still in experimentation mode and not yet budgeting token spend as a durable line item.
“I think it's going to be some time before anybody is going to give you a really credible answer to that question.” ▶ 40:24
Talent flywheel versus independent media 4
The New York Times newsroom has roughly a thousand more journalists than 13 years ago and holds the largest collection of journalists in its history even excluding The Athletic and Wirecutter, a scale Levien treats as the core competitive asset.
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesThe AthleticWirecutter
So what: Headcount scale is the barrier to entry Levien is betting on; it is also the fixed cost that makes NYT unattractive to replicate and hard to unwind if growth stalls.
“there are like a thousand more journalists in the New York Times newsroom today than when I got there 13 years ago” ▶ 16:37
The NYT retention model is peer-and-infrastructure based rather than compensation based: journalists stay for the best editors, lawyers, security, graphics and photographers around them, with Jonathan Swan and Ezra Klein cited as evidence.
framework🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesJonathan SwanMaggie HabermanEzra KleinSubstack
So what: If the moat is support infrastructure rather than pay, NYT's talent costs stay contained even as Substack economics improve — but the model self-selects against solo-brand stars.
“The best journalists in their career at the top of their game, coming to do the best work of their careers, supported by the best editors, the best partners.” ▶ 17:02
Levien is skeptical that meaningful journalism is being produced outside journalistic organizations, saying it has not been her experience as a media consumer that much of it exists — a direct pushback on the independent-creator-media thesis.
contrarian🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highSubstackTikTokXThe New York Times
So what: Management does not view creator platforms as a competing supply of reported journalism, so expect NYT to keep hiring institutionally rather than acquiring independent brands.
“I will say it's not been my experience as a media consumer that you're seeing a ton of kind of journalistic work, outside of the journalistic organizations.” ▶ 21:20
Joe Weisenthal argues the New York Times' success has made it effectively the monopsony buyer of top journalistic talent, draining roles like a Washington reporter from local outlets such as the Dallas Morning News because local papers cannot compete at internet scale.
thesis🗣 Joe Weisenthalconviction: mediumThe New York TimesDallas Morning Newslocal newsCraigslistGoogle News
