So what: A structural argument that NYT's wins are non-replicable industry-wide — bearish for local news assets and for any 'NYT playbook' turnaround stories in regional media.
“the New York Times, with its success, has become the sort of like, yeah, like the monopsony buyer of great talent in the news, business, etc.” ▶ 57:12
Video push and its ROI question 5
Levien frames NYT video not as a substitute for reading or listening but as an attack on a separate, giant watch-native audience currently getting news-adjacent entertainment on TikTok, YouTube and Instagram — a market NYT plays in only nascently today.
thesis🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesYouTubeTikTokInstagram
So what: The video bet is a TAM-expansion bet, so judge it on incremental new-audience reach rather than on engagement cannibalized from existing subscribers.
“this is not a replacement behavior for reading and listening. This is the times making a big moves into a market that we play in only in a nascent way today” ▶ 29:29
Levien's format thesis from print-to-digital, web-to-mobile and the audio transition is that the old format never goes away — readers keep reading and listeners keep listening — so new formats add audience rather than replacing it.
framework🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesThe Dailypodcasts
So what: A reusable model for forecasting media format transitions: additive layering, not substitution, which argues against writing off legacy formats too early.
“it has been our experience that the the old format does not go away. If you listen, you're going to listen.” ▶ 28:17
Levien, who ran NYT's commercial business during the 2010s pivot to video, calls that era a cynical attempt to capture Facebook ad dollars, and distinguishes today's push as engagement-driven — targeting millions of people engaging daily rather than ad impressions.
contrarian🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesFacebookvideo advertising
So what: The test for whether this video cycle differs from the last is whether it converts to subscriptions and habit, not whether it sells video ad inventory.
“I ran our commercial business the when the pivot to video happened, that was a cynical attempt to get ad dollars without regard to the thing I said at the very beginning, which is, can you get millions of people to engage with the thing?” ▶ 30:19
NYT is staffing its video expansion largely from existing employees and adding capability later, which limits the incremental cost of the video build relative to a hire-first approach.
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York Times
So what: Directly relevant to the margin worry driving the post-earnings selloff: video opex should scale more slowly than video output.
“The push and video we're making now, we are making it is largely with people who were already at the times. We're adding capability later” ▶ 56:05
Joe Weisenthal notes the NYT stock has sold off after each of the last two earnings reports, including the one the day before this interview, on anxiety about costly video production — an investor concern Levien answers by invoking the long-game benefit of family control rather than by quantifying video ROI.
data_point🗣 Joe Weisenthalconviction: mediumThe New York TimesNYT stock
So what: Management declined to give a video payback framework, so the cost-versus-audience debate stays unresolved into the next print — a repeatable post-earnings volatility setup.
Capital allocation, family control, and M&A discipline 5
Levien insists hard news journalism is NYT's most economically value-creating product 'writ large' and explicitly rejects the widespread view that serious news is a loss leader subsidized by games and sports.
contrarian🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesNYT GamesThe Athletic
So what: If true, NYT's earnings quality is more durable than a games-carries-news reading implies; if the claim is directional rather than accounting, segment-level disclosure is where to test it.
“sort of hard core independent news journalism, particularly about serious topics, is like a loss leader. You do it because you have to do it. You do it for the mission.” ▶ 11:14
Coverage spending decisions at NYT are made by editors, often below the executive editor level — the international editor decided to keep roughly 70 people in and out of Ukraine last year, five years into the war — with the CEO's job defined as making the company profitable enough that no editor has to weigh cost.
framework🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesUkraineDeclan WalshDRCSudanMyanmar
So what: Editorial cost is structurally uncapped by design, so margin expansion at NYT must come from revenue growth rather than from newsroom cost discipline.
“who gets to decide how many people we have on the ground in Ukraine. I think we had 70 last year in and out.” ▶ 13:49
Levien attributes NYT's ability to make large, slow-maturing bets to the family control structure, which exists for two stated reasons: keeping journalism independent even of the company's own commercial interest, and enabling a long game.
causal🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesSulzberger family
So what: Dual-class control is the explicit reason management can absorb post-earnings selloffs — investors buying NYT are buying a structure that will not respond to quarterly pressure.
“Why does that control structure exist? Two reasons. One, keep the journalism independent and in service to nobody's interest, not even our own commercial interest. And two, it allows us to play long game.” ▶ 24:46
Levien does not rule out another acquisition on the scale of The Athletic but says the return-on-investment bar for inorganic additions is 'really high,' signaling a strong preference for building in-house.
thesis🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: mediumThe New York TimesThe Athletic
So what: Lowers the probability of a large near-term NYT acquisition and supports a capital-return or organic-investment base case.
“I would say to, people who think about our economics listening. The bar is really high for return on investment.” ▶ 55:40
The Athletic integration went far better than expected culturally: NYT acquired roughly 450 journalists with only a tiny business team, has grown that newsroom by about 100 people, added a substantial editing layer it lacked, and kept it operating as an independent brand under publisher David Perpich.
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe AthleticThe New York TimesSteven GinsburgWashington PostDavid Perpich
So what: A concrete integration playbook — buy the newsroom, keep the brand separate, layer on editing and business infrastructure — that lowers execution risk on any future NYT acquisition.
“we acquired like a giant newsroom with a tiny little business team, which was super interesting” ▶ 53:11
Audience capture, trust, and newsroom control 5
Levien flatly rejects audience capture as a business model, saying NYT wants a giant engaged audience but will not chase one, and argues the total NYT audience already reflects the population at large more than the liberal stereotype implies.
thesis🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York Times
So what: Management's growth math assumes political breadth; if subscriber growth concentrates ideologically, the TAM story shrinks materially.
“We do not chase audiences. We want a giant audience. We want a giant, engaged audience.” ▶ 33:43
Levien extends the no-capture principle to commercial content, saying Wirecutter will name the most comfortable mattress even when a different mattress company is a big advertiser — treating advertiser-independent reviews as the source of the vertical's value.
causal🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highWirecutterThe New York Times
So what: Trust-first affiliate/review economics is the model NYT is defending against AI-generated review slop; expect no shift toward advertiser-influenced commerce content.
“if the mattress everybody thinks is better or cooler isn't the one that our testers say is most comfortable, we're going to say the truth about that, too, even if that other mattress company is the big advertiser” ▶ 34:40
Levien argues verified provenance is the emerging consumer demand in an environment saturated with slop and disinformation — people want to know what they receive is true, that a fair process produced it, and to see the receipts.
thesis🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesAIdisinformation
So what: Positions verified-provenance journalism as a countercyclical asset whose value rises with AI content volume — the central bull case for premium news brands.
“People want to know the thing that I'm getting is true. They want to know that a fair process was pursued to get that truth. They'd like to see the receipts of that process.” ▶ 34:06
To manage internal ideological capture after episodes like the Tom Cotton op-ed backlash and Bari Weiss's departure, NYT built what Levien calls the largest trust and standards team in the industry, much bigger than five or six years ago, plus monthly all-company meetings that did not exist before her tenure.
data_point🗣 Meredith Kopit Levienconviction: highThe New York TimesBari WeissTom CottonJoe KahnA.G. Sulzberger
So what: Institutional overhead is being spent specifically to prevent staff-driven editorial capture — a real cost line that also protects the trust asset the whole subscription model rests on.
“We have a trust and standards team that is absolutely the largest in the industry.” ▶ 51:28
The hosts conclude there may be room for only one bundled 'one-stop shop' in English-language media, and that the open question is how durable and how large the educated, curious, open-minded cohort is before it splinters into ideologically or personally satisfying niches.
thesis🗣 Joe Weisenthal and Tracy Allowayconviction: mediumThe New York Timeslocal newsmedia
So what: Frames the key long-term NYT risk as cohort durability rather than competition — watch net adds among younger, vertical-native consumers for the first evidence of splintering.
“it may be that there's only room for like one, one stop shop for content” ▶ 59:23

Hidden Forces · Demetri Kofinas 1 episode(s) · 32 insights

The Internet Will Die and So Will You | John West61 min32 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Wall Street Journal computational journalist John West argues the web is structurally ephemeral (link rot, dead open standards like RSS and Google Reader) and that its modern history is a transfer of agency from users to platforms, consummated the moment an algorithm was inserted between follower and followee. He and Demetri Kofinas trace how the ad-funded, engagement-maximizing model degraded search and sense-making, ask whether AI escapes or repeats that 'enshittification' arc, and close on epistemic collapse — why norms cannot keep pace with the technology, why people cannot tolerate perpetual uncertainty, and why 'neutral algorithm' is an incoherent idea.
Link rot and the web's structural impermanence · 3Human agency to platform agency · 4Open standards vs. closed feeds and switching costs · 4Ad model, two masters, and the enshittification of AI · 5Vibe coding and the small-web revival · 4Search decay and human-sourced trust signals · 4Epistemic collapse, norms lag, and algorithmic values · 8
Link rot and the web's structural impermanence 3
John West argues the internet's reputation as permanent and indelible is backwards: ephemerality is built into the web's structure, so digital archives and 'permanent records' decay by default rather than persist.
thesis🗣 John Westconviction: highinternetlink rotweb archives
So what: Any business, index, or dataset built on the assumption that web content persists (citation graphs, training corpora, evidence trails) carries an unpriced decay rate.
“It can be that but also it can be a deeply ephemeral place and that ephemerality is baked into the very structure of the web.” ▶ 7:32
West defines a two-stage decay mechanism for the web: sites first suffer content or link drift — a URL redirecting somewhere unexpected, at worst a Bitcoin scam — and then go fully dark to a 404.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highlink rotcontent drift404 errors
So what: Drift precedes death, so redirect-pattern monitoring is a leading indicator of link-graph and archive rot before hard 404s appear.
“Linkrod is the phenomenon where a website goes dark where you click on that blue underlined link of text and instead of taking you to a new website it takes you to a 404 page, an error page.” ▶ 6:45
West frames the internet as regenerative rather than static — it persists only through active maintenance and attention, and decays toward entropy without it, a gardening rather than a wilderness model.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highinternetdigital preservation
So what: Maintenance, not creation, is the scarce input in digital infrastructure; budget archiving and stewardship as recurring opex, not a one-time capex.
“just as nature will produce things if left to its own devices, we have a responsibility when we garden to make the soil richer and to shape that natural process into something that is deeply unnatural actually” ▶ 15:09
Human agency to platform agency 4
West's central thesis is that the modern internet's defining story is a transfer of agency from humans to platforms — an outside force with incentives that run against human thriving.
thesis🗣 John Westconviction: highsocial media platformsFacebookuser agency
So what: Frames platform regulation and product design fights as agency-allocation questions, the axis on which future antitrust and design-standard pressure will land.
“the story of the internet in its modern incarnation is a shift from human agency to platform agency” ▶ 9:27
West claims platforms hold a mechanistic model of human behavior that then reshapes users' own sense of what is possible online, and that users bringing their full selves to social media would damage those companies' bottom lines.
causal🗣 John Westconviction: mediumsocial media platformsMarilynne Robinsonengagement models
So what: Implies a structural conflict between richer, more deliberate usage and monetization — 'time well spent' style products face an inherent revenue ceiling on ad-funded platforms.
“these platforms which are deeply mechanistic in how they think about our behavior and how they think about us and that that infects our own thinking about what is possible and what is desirable online” ▶ 10:50
West and Kofinas locate online 'homelessness' in the fact that users do not own the communities they belong to — displacement, not deletion, is the core harm of walled gardens.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highFacebook groupsRedditblogospherewalled gardens
So what: Portability and data-ownership features are the direct answer to the churn risk platforms create; watch protocol-based social products positioning there.
“the displacement online that we feel is about a loss of agency. I think it comes back to that loss of agency.” ▶ 32:34
West uses the Google Reader shutdown as the canonical case of platform-caused displacement — killing one client destroyed the shared street layout of the blogosphere and scattered its residents.
anecdote🗣 John Westconviction: highGoogle ReaderRSSblogosphereGoogle
So what: Deprecation of a dominant client, not of the underlying standard, is what kills an ecosystem — a risk to price in any single-vendor-dependent open protocol.
“when Google Reader went away, they ripped up 12th Street and all of a sudden, you know, the people who lived there had to find new digs” ▶ 32:28
Open standards vs. closed feeds and switching costs 4
West credits RSS — an open feed standard — with enabling the blogging boom, and blames its replacement by closed feeds such as Facebook's News Feed and X for collapsing the web's interoperability.
causal🗣 John Westconviction: highRSSFacebook News FeedXTwitterblogspodcasts
So what: Open-standard distribution (RSS still powers podcasts) is the last unmediated channel to an audience; its persistence is why podcasting resisted platform capture.
West singles out X as more hostile to external links than Twitter was, i.e. platforms now actively suppress outbound traffic rather than merely failing to promote it.
data_point🗣 John Westconviction: highXTwitterFacebookreferral traffic
So what: Publishers and media businesses dependent on social referral face a structural, not cyclical, decline in traffic — direct and RSS-style channels are the hedge.
“or even Twitter now X which is even more hostile to external links” ▶ 19:51
West identifies the precise inflection in social media as the moment an algorithm was inserted between follower and followee — not the arrival of social networking itself — and says that is when the center of gravity shifted.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highFacebookMySpaceTik Tokrecommendation algorithms
So what: Dates the regime change for anyone modeling attention markets: pre-algorithmic reach was a function of the follower graph, after it of ranking, which the platform can tax at will.
“a shift that I would want to draw a lot of attention to is the moment when it went from being about, you know, I follow you and I see your posts to I follow you and an algorithm sits between you and me and decides which posts I see” ▶ 34:30
West, who says he is normally predisposed to think poorly of markets, argues competition would improve the web and that an open, portable follower graph could unlock a generative new era — but switching costs make Facebook's and LinkedIn's inertia nearly unbeatable.
contrarian🗣 John Westconviction: mediumFacebookLinkedInfollower graphswitching costs
So what: Mandated graph portability is the single intervention that would reopen social networking to new entrants — the policy lever to watch for incumbent valuation risk.
“that switching cost you just described is like makes it really really hard to overcome the inertia of Facebook or LinkedIn or whatever it is” ▶ 35:26
Ad model, two masters, and the enshittification of AI 5
West argues recommendation systems are structurally conflicted — servants of two masters, satisfying users' real desires while driving revenue for the company that built them — and that the two objectives are frequently in tension.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highrecommendation algorithmsRSS readersad-funded platforms
So what: A clean test for any feed or assistant product: whose objective function is it optimizing? Subscription-funded rankers are the only ones where the conflict is absent.
“they are the servants of two masters all too often. They are the servants of the users who have real desires, but they are really driving revenue for whatever company has built them.” ▶ 36:17
Kofinas frames the last 20-30 years of the web as a shift from serving users to serving advertisers, with users as inventory to be harvested; West agrees.
thesis🗣 Demetri Kofinas (host)conviction: highdigital advertisingGoogleFacebooksocial media
So what: Keeps the advertising business model, not the technology, as the causal variable behind product degradation — the same test to apply to AI monetization.
“we're there as sheep to be sheared, as cows to be milked for these giant corporations” ▶ 36:48
West says AI has not yet undergone the 'enshittification' that platform technologies did, but that its monetization model is simply not yet built — implying today's high AI product quality is a pre-monetization artifact, not a durable property.
prediction🗣 John Westconviction: mediumAI companiesCory Doctorowchatbotsplatform monetization
So what: The bull case on AI assistant quality should be discounted for the monetization phase that has not yet arrived; watch for the first ad or commerce injection as the regime marker.
“I think that we have not yet seen the to use Cory Dtoro's word which I think is a useful word initification of AI in quite the same way that we have of the platform technologies” ▶ 37:53
West names the specific degradation mechanism to watch for: an AI company tweaking the model so a chatbot's recommendations hawk whatever product it is being paid to advertise.
prediction🗣 John Westconviction: mediumAI chatbotsadvertisingmodel alignment
So what: Native, unlabeled commercial bias inside model outputs would be far harder to detect than sponsored search links — a live regulatory and trust risk for assistant products.
“what if we just tweak the model to you know hawk whatever the product is that we're advertising for in line with the recommendations that they give in a chatbot” ▶ 39:57
West expects AI to become very expensive once users pay the full token cost of what they consume, implying current usage is subsidized.
prediction🗣 John Westconviction: mediumAI companiesinference coststokens
So what: Ad-supported AI is the likely resolution of the subsidy gap — the same path that pushed the web from user-serving to advertiser-serving.
“what's going to happen when we actually have to pay the full token costs of what we're doing. I mean I I think it's going to be very expensive.” ▶ 40:06
Vibe coding and the small-web revival 4
West says AI's most exciting effect is a revival of experimental, personal web-making: he vibe-coded Mixtab, a tiny private social site that turns friends' bookmarks into a shared newspaper edition, in about a week despite lacking the time to build it manually.
anecdote🗣 John Westconviction: highAI coding toolsvibe codingMixtabsmall web
So what: Collapsing build cost for micro-social software creates demand for cheap hosting, personal-app tooling, and small-audience products that never cleared the old economics.
West argues the current 'why not just make it' mood among AI-assisted builders feels like the early web, framing AI as a re-decentralizing force at the creation layer even while platforms remain concentrated at the distribution layer.
thesis🗣 John Westconviction: mediumAIearly webGeocitiesindie web
So what: Cheap creation plus captured distribution is the central tension of the next web cycle — the bottleneck value shifts to whoever controls discovery.
“there's this kind of like well why not just make it feeling that a lot of people are having right now that really to me feels a lot like the early web did” ▶ 39:42
West predicts AI will never live up to either its detractors' or its proponents' promises and will instead land somewhere weirder — a middle-path stance against both the doom and the AGI-imminent camps.
contrarian🗣 John Westconviction: highAIAGI discourse
So what: Argues against positioning around either the collapse or the singularity scenario; the tradeable path is specific capability adoption, not the extremes.
“I think AI is never going to live up to the promises of its detractors or its proponents.” ▶ 38:50
West dismisses 'will AI achieve superintelligence' as a category error and possibly the wrong question, arguing the useful and actionable question is which specific capabilities these systems will have — noting AI is a bundle of very different technologies packaged under one label.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highAIEmily BenderThe AI ConWittgensteinsuperintelligence
So what: A capability-level rather than intelligence-level analysis is the one that maps to revenue lines and displacement risk; superintelligence debates carry no actionable information.
“I think that it is not a very useful question if AI will achieve super intelligence. I think it is a very useful question to wonder like what are the specific capabilities of artificial intelligence systems going to look like?” ▶ 44:00
Search decay and human-sourced trust signals 4
West says Google search was already dying before AI: results have degraded into sponsored material at the top, with the best results now found by scrolling to the bottom — an inversion of how Google used to work.
data_point🗣 John Westconviction: highGooglesearch advertisingPageRank
So what: Search-quality erosion predates AI competition, so Google's share loss is partly self-inflicted rather than purely a chatbot substitution story.
“I used to go on Google and get great search results. So now I just get a bunch of sponsored crap and I always try to remember to scroll to the bottom of the page where the best results are rather than the top” ▶ 40:45
West reads the mass habit of appending 'Reddit' to Google queries as evidence that users trust human social signal over a heavily gamified ranking algorithm, and that the internet remains fundamentally a social place.
causal🗣 John Westconviction: highRedditGoogleSEOPageRank
So what: Human-verified content pools become the scarce, licensable asset in an AI-saturated web — the structural basis for Reddit-style data value.
“people have been appending Reddit to their search queries in an effort to get the Reddit results for things because that's more trusted often times than the results that are just natively given back from Google” ▶ 40:58
West warns that as more of the internet is written by AI and LLMs are trained on the internet, the feedback loop threatens both the corpus and the humans entangled with it — a self-consuming knowledge supply.
causal🗣 John Westconviction: mediumlarge language modelsAI-generated contenttraining data
So what: Pre-AI human text becomes a dated, non-renewable asset; owners of verified human corpora hold pricing power over model builders.
West holds that meaning is made by humans and is not an independent fact of the world, while information may be external — making meaning-making the essential human function AI cannot simply assume.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highAIinformation theorymeaning
So what: Defines where human labor stays defensible as generation costs fall: judgment and meaning attribution rather than information retrieval.
“I think meaning is something we bring to the world. It is not an independent fact of the world outside of it.” ▶ 42:58
Epistemic collapse, norms lag, and algorithmic values 8
West's explanation for why past technology panics were overblown is that societies had time to build social norms around use — and he argues the internet, social media and AI now move far faster than norms can form, making this cycle genuinely different from television, the novel, or writing.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highAIsocial mediatelevisionPlatosocial norms
So what: The norms-lag framework predicts regulatory and backlash risk scales with adoption speed — the reason AI's compressed timeline raises policy risk above prior technology waves.
“the internet and AI and social media all moves so much faster than our norms are capable of keeping up with” ▶ 47:13
West says he is glad the modern distribution system for conspiracy theories did not exist during 9/11, and predicts that in today's ecosystem such theories would spread farther, faster and stranger.
prediction🗣 John Westconviction: high9/11conspiracy theoriessocial mediaIraq war
So what: Implies the next mass-casualty or crisis event produces far more severe narrative fragmentation, a tail risk for policy response and market-relevant crisis coordination.
“I think we would see that spread farther and faster and stranger than before.” ▶ 46:25
Kofinas argues institutional gaslighting plus shattered priors — the 2008 financial crisis and COVID revealing long-held beliefs to be false — makes populations reprogrammable into equally absurd new realities, because people cannot live in perpetual uncertainty.
causal🗣 Demetri Kofinas (host)conviction: high2008 financial crisisCOVIDconspiracy theoriesNaomi Klein
So what: Credibility shocks at institutions are followed by belief-migration, not agnosticism — a repeatable pattern for anticipating post-crisis political and narrative regime shifts.
“people in the end cannot live in a perpetual state of uncertainty. They need to believe in something that's concrete even if it's not true.” ▶ 48:24
West argues knowledge production is an underappreciated, thorny human process — at the Wall Street Journal it is extremely hard to say only true things and not even imply false ones — which is why claims that AI will 'solve journalism' by being unbiased miss what the work actually is.
contrarian🗣 John Westconviction: highWall Street JournaljournalismAI companies
So what: AI-replaces-journalism theses underrate the embodied judgment layer; the defensible media asset is verified process, not article volume.
“you have to be an embodied person with other embodied people at some level and you have to be in the muck trying your best to produce real knowledge from that” ▶ 49:49
West offers a process-over-outcome trust protocol: trust not that a speaker is right but that they are running a process that is more often right than not — which Kofinas notes lets you assess credibility without domain expertise.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highepistemicsjournalismcredibility assessment
So what: A portable due-diligence heuristic for evaluating analysts, research providers and AI outputs when you lack subject-matter knowledge.
“I try to trust not that the people who are saying the thing are say are right but I try to trust that the people are when they are doing a process that is more often right than not.” ▶ 50:18
West identifies 'vice signaling' as the current online status mechanic — an inversion of virtue signaling where people gain credibility by saying increasingly distressing things they may not even believe, and end up believing them later.
framework🗣 John Westconviction: highsocial mediavice signalingonline radicalization
So what: Explains why platform discourse ratchets toward extremity independent of politics — brand-safety risk for advertisers rises structurally, not cyclically.
“you have this kind of weird inverse of it where people actually gain credibility by saying increasingly distressing things that even they might not think are okay” ▶ 53:52
West's Leaves of Grass experiment — embedding Walt Whitman stanza by stanza and serving more of whatever he dwelled on — unexpectedly kept him engaged, demonstrating that engagement optimization is nearly content-neutral yet has severe content consequences.
anecdote🗣 John Westconviction: highengagement algorithmsembeddingsWalt WhitmanAI models
So what: Engagement mechanics work on any corpus, so blaming content rather than ranking design misdiagnoses the problem — and the same mechanics will transfer directly into AI interfaces.
“the longer I spent I dwelled on each stanza, the more stanzas I would get like that. And I thought it was just a dumb exercise that was not going to work. And it actually kept me engaged.” ▶ 58:08
West rejects the idea of a neutral algorithm outright: any constructed ranking system necessarily carries bias — toward engagement, friends, or something else — and will amplify some voices over others by construction.
contrarian🗣 John Westconviction: highrecommendation algorithmscontent moderationplatform governance
So what: Undermines 'neutral platform' defenses in both regulation and litigation; the enforceable question becomes which values a ranker encodes, not whether it is biased.
“it is not the case that there is a neutral algorithm that would equally amplify voices on the left and the right” ▶ 59:11

Perun 1 episode(s) · 31 insights

I let the Internet Design A (Bad) Military - Orbital Strikes to Battleships, Arming Emutopia Special60 min31 insights2026-08-09
▶ Watch on YouTube
Perun runs a deliberately comedic procurement thought experiment - crowdsourced equipment picks (M7 rifle, T-14 Armata, Queen Elizabeth carriers, an aviation battleship, XL-UUV 'submarine carriers', orbital tungsten rods, space marines) for the fictional middle power Emutopia - and reverse-engineers plausible-sounding justifications for each. The serious payload is procurement economics: requirement wording rather than the tender determines the winner, individually defensible buys hollow out a force when summed, and opportunity cost plus creative reuse of existing platforms usually beats buying the shiny thing.
Requirement-Setting as the Real Selection Lever · 5Infantry Small-Arms Weight vs Penetration · 3Armour Economics and the High-Low Ground Mix · 5Capital Ships and the Hollowed-Out Middle Fleet · 4XL-UUVs as Attritable Naval Mass · 4Orbital Kinetic Strike Cost Case · 5Dual-Use Space Programs and Cheap Resilience · 5
Requirement-Setting as the Real Selection Lever 5
Perun argues defence procurement outcomes are usually predetermined by how the initial requirement is written rather than by bias or corruption inside the evaluation process, since a single line item on dimensions, performance, armament, manufacturing arrangements or compatibility can decide the winner long before any individual design is assessed.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highT-14 Armatadefence procurementEmutopia
So what: To predict a defence tender's winner, read the requirements document rather than the shortlist - favoured suppliers are visible in the spec long before award.
“I didn't have to specifically say, I want to rig the selection process in favor of the T-14 Armata. I just had to set much more reasonable-sounding parameters” ▶ 55:27
Perun shows that 'risk reduction' clauses requiring a selected system to already be in service with a major military, or to have seen combat, are common-sense-sounding filters that in practice hand a competition to a predetermined incumbent regardless of the design's merit.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highM7T-14 Armatadefence procurement
So what: In-service and combat-proven clauses are structural moats for established primes and a signal that a nominally open tender is not open.
“This reduces Imutopia's risk because no major military would ever purchase a dud” ▶ 9:31
Perun frames the central requirements-writing tension as being prescriptive enough to steer industry without writing out creative solutions or discovering late that the stated requirement was not the actual need, citing the B-2's 'wouldn't it be great' low-level bombing capability as costing multiple years of delay and engineering sacrifices.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highB-2 Spiritdefence procurement
So what: The requirement versus nice-to-have boundary is where schedule slip and cost growth are seeded - a practical screen for program risk.
Perun warns that individually defensible purchases become incoherent when summed, because tunnel vision on single platforms without a force-level concept leaves planners backward-engineering a strategy around capital ships they can no longer escort.
causal🗣 Perunconviction: highQueen Elizabeth classXL UUVfrigatesdestroyers
So what: Assess national force plans at portfolio level - a plan that hollows the escort tier tends to force a corrective procurement cycle later.
“It is entirely possible to put up a piece of equipment that by itself might come with a great sales pitch and an excellent stat sheet, and yet it not be the correct fit for the overall force.” ▶ 57:19
Perun advocates a 'recycle before replace' logic check, arguing repurposing existing platforms is usually more efficient than wholesale replacement because replacement drags in a new sustainment plan and training program, and citing the Hawker Typhoon becoming a ground-attack aircraft and the Messerschmitt 110 becoming a night fighter.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highHawker TyphoonMesserschmitt 110XL UUV
So what: Upgrade and role-change programs are a persistently underrated revenue pool relative to headline new-build platforms.
“before you replace, it's decent to throw in a logic check to see if you can first recycle” ▶ 58:27
Infantry Small-Arms Weight vs Penetration 3
Perun argues the decisive procurement and logistics consequence of shifting from 5.56 NATO to a higher-pressure round like the M7's is that larger rounds are larger, forcing soldiers either to significantly cut their ammunition load or spend more weight to carry the same round count.
causal🗣 Perunconviction: highM75.56 NATOUS Army
So what: Next-generation rifle programs carry a hidden logistics tax in magazine capacity, load carriage and resupply volume that never appears in the headline unit price.
“it's that larger rounds are larger” ▶ 6:16
Perun notes that abandoning 5.56 NATO forfeits ammunition cross-compatibility with a significant number of allied forces plus access to massive global stockpiles and production capacity - an interoperability and surge-supply cost that a national cartridge change quietly imposes.
causal🗣 Perunconviction: high5.56 NATOM7NATO
So what: Cartridge changes fragment the allied ammunition base and create long-tail demand for new production capacity instead of drawing on existing surplus.
Perun contends Ukraine's experience argues against heavier infantry rifles, because long dismounted movements make weight a premium and longer-range lethality against infantry there is delivered by FPV drones and artillery rather than small arms, so spare carrying capacity is better spent on drone batteries or a CQB shotgun.
contrarian🗣 Perunconviction: highUkraineFPV dronesartilleryM7
So what: The demand signal favours infantry drone kit and load carriage over next-generation rifle programs - treat NGSW-style budgets as contestable.
Armour Economics and the High-Low Ground Mix 5
Perun highlights that Russia planned 2,300 T-14 Armatas by 2025 but built only a handful, with hesitation predating the Ukraine war - then-Deputy PM Yuri Borisov said in 2018 that money would go to upgraded older designs instead, claiming upgraded T-72s beat German, French and US MBTs on price, efficiency and quality with a budget ten times smaller than NATO's.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: highT-14 ArmataUralvagonzavodYuri BorisovT-72RussiaNATO
So what: A canonical case of a clean-sheet next-generation platform losing to upgrade economics - a structural tailwind for retrofit and modernisation contractors.
“Why flood our military with Armatas? The T-72s are in great demand in the markets.” ▶ 11:09
Perun demonstrates that a weight cap justified by bridges, airlift or landing craft, plus an uncrewed-turret requirement, minimum gun calibre, hard-kill active protection and a drone-swatting remote weapon station, is enough to eliminate the Leopard 2A8, a modernised Abrams and Challenger 3 while leaving the T-14 Armata technically compliant.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highLeopard 2A8M1 AbramsChallenger 3T-14 ArmataAfghanit
So what: Weight limits and turret architecture clauses are the most powerful discriminators in MBT tenders and worth tracking in any live armour competition.
Perun notes demand for rugged civilian vehicles on the Ukrainian battlefield grew large enough that the Ukrainian MOD ran its own procurements for thousands of them to augment volunteer-supplied vehicles, with technicals used as guided-missile launch platforms and as anti-aircraft carriers in mobile fire groups downing Shahed-style drones.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: highUkraineUkrainian MODShahedtechnicals
So what: Confirms a durable state-level procurement channel for commercial 4x4s and pickup conversions in high-intensity war, not just an improvised curiosity.
Perun argues mass corrugated-iron shelters - real shelters plus layered dummy shelters over aircraft, vehicles and stores - are an extremely cheap counter to satellite-based targeting because they impose a punishing cost exchange on an attacker's ballistic missile inventory.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: mediumsatellite reconnaissanceballistic missilesEmutopia
So what: Low-cost concealment and decoy infrastructure is an underpriced defensive investment relative to active missile defence.
“they better be building billions in ballistic missiles to blow up a bunch of bulk buildings from Bunnings” ▶ 18:09
Perun justifies buying roughly 100 Lamborghinis with only about 12 used for actual reconnaissance on the grounds that the real problem is recruitment and retention, arguing volunteer militaries must spend heavily on attracting and keeping personnel so a few cruise missiles' worth of spending on a recruiting hook can be rational.
thesis🗣 Perunconviction: mediumLamborghiniEmutopiavolunteer militaries
So what: Personnel attraction and retention is a real, growing and often unbudgeted competitor for hardware dollars in all-volunteer forces.
“the problem we're trying to solve here isn't actually reconnaissance, it's recruitment and retention” ▶ 16:54
Capital Ships and the Hollowed-Out Middle Fleet 4
Perun identifies three binding constraints on a middle power buying two Queen Elizabeth class carriers, each over 280 m and 80,000 tons: shipyards and workforce to build them on a reasonable timeline, costs that would cannibalise other critical naval investment unless stretched over an unreasonable period, and oppressive crew requirements.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highQueen Elizabeth classF-35Emutopia
So what: Carrier ambitions for mid-tier navies are gated by yards and crews more than sticker price - watch drydock and workforce capacity as the binding constraint.
Perun floats a multinational 'time-share' carrier fleet across the UK, Canada and Emutopia - one procurement and maintenance plan for four hulls, deep maintenance in the UK, some servicing in the other two theatres - allowing one carrier permanently deployed and surge to two or three, at the cost of massive political complication over who gets the ships when.
thesis🗣 Perunconviction: exploratoryQueen Elizabeth classPrince of WalesUnited KingdomCanada
So what: Joint multinational capital-ship ownership would concentrate sustainment revenue in a single lead yard while breaking the one-nation-one-hull cost ceiling.
Perun exposes the standard multi-role funding trick: a Swiss Army knife combatant is affordable only by cutting the specialised LHA/LHD, frigate, destroyer, hospital ship and command ship that each did the job better, and concentrating those capabilities in one hull removes the option of having them in several places at once.
causal🗣 Perunconviction: highLHALHDfrigatesdestroyershospital ship
So what: Multi-mission consolidation pitches should be stress-tested against dispersion value - the offsets funding them usually destroy fleet flexibility.
Perun characterises the resulting fleet as top and bottom-heavy, with displacement and manpower concentrated in carriers and battleships and the remainder in underwater drones, leaving minesweepers, patrol boats, corvettes, frigates and destroyers short-funded while every remaining escort is tied to carrier protection.
causal🗣 Perunconviction: highfrigatesdestroyersminesweeperscorvettesXL UUV
So what: Escort and mine-warfare shortfalls are the predictable second-order consequence of capital-ship-led naval plans and a recurring source of later emergency procurement.
XL-UUVs as Attritable Naval Mass 4
Perun proposes turning extra-large UUVs such as Australia's Ghost Shark and America's Orca into drone carriers via a payload module launching FPVs or loitering munitions, arguing it is an incremental ask because navies already treat XL-UUVs as multi-purpose payload carriers for sensors, robotic payloads and mines at comparatively low cost versus crewed submarines.
thesis🗣 Perunconviction: mediumGhost SharkOrcaXL UUVAustraliaUnited StatesChina
So what: Payload-module ecosystems rather than the hulls are where XL-UUV value accrues - watch modular payload contracts around Ghost Shark and Orca.
Perun notes Ukraine has repeatedly launched FPVs from small surface drones, trading warhead size for reusability, range and precision, and used them against soft, valuable, hard-to-conceal coastal targets like radars and air defence installations that must sit in specific places and are very fragile.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: highUkraineUSVFPV dronesair defenceradars
So what: The drone-carrying-drone pattern is battle-proven and is the template for the next generation of uncrewed maritime strike.
Perun argues uncrewed submarines are far easier to surge in wartime than crewed ones because trained and experienced crews cannot be rush-built, so designing XL-UUVs for mass production - tooling in advance, stockpiled long-lead items, maximum commercial off-the-shelf content - lets a state 'hit print' on a navy given warning of a crisis.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highXL UUVsubmarinesCOTS
So what: Warm tooling and long-lead-item stockpiling is the concrete fundable form of mobilisation readiness, a spending category distinct from platform buys.
Perun frames a drone-heavy navy as explicitly optimised for deterrence by punishment rather than denial, applying lessons from Ukraine, Iran and the Houthis with better technology and more scale to make opposing ports prohibitively risky for civilian traffic and pin down disproportionate anti-submarine warfare assets - while conceding there is only so much disaster relief you can do with a robotic submarine.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highUkraineIranHouthisASWXL UUV
So what: Punishment-based maritime deterrence favours cheap distributed effectors and mines over blue-water escorts, but sacrifices the peacetime presence roles that dominate political tasking.
“We don't try and go down the road of deterrence by denial. We don't have the resources for it. Instead, the name of the game is deterrence by punishment.” ▶ 34:19
Orbital Kinetic Strike Cost Case 5
Perun relays RAND's 2002 'Space Weapons Earth Wars' constraint that long, slender, heavy, non-explosive orbital penetrators are useful only against targets vulnerable to vertical penetration - tall buildings, missile silos, ships and hardened aircraft shelters - and not against runways, deeply buried bunkers, bridges or low, long buildings.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: highRANDUS Air Forcekinetic bombardmentmissile silos
So what: Rod-from-God target sets are far narrower than popular perception, which caps the achievable military value of any such program.
“Suitable weapons would include tall buildings, missile silos, ships, and hardened aircraft shelters, but not runways, deeply buried bunkers, bridges, and low, long buildings.” ▶ 39:32
Perun cites RAND's sizing example of a roughly 1 m, 100 kg rod penetrating about 1.5 m of steel, versus other studies contemplating 100-plus-ton rods, and notes global responsive coverage implies an absentee ratio of roughly six satellites for every one usefully on station.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: highRANDkinetic bombardmentlow Earth orbit
So what: The absentee ratio is the hidden multiplier that makes orbital strike constellations economically brutal, and the same logic applies to any responsive-coverage space architecture.
“Space-based kinetic energy weapons directed at surface targets are very difficult to defend against because of their very high velocity and very brief flight through the atmosphere.” ▶ 37:58
Perun argues orbital kinetic strike is dominated on cost by terrestrial alternatives in 2026 - hard-to-intercept ballistic missiles such as Russia's RS-24 and China's DF-21 plus hypersonic glide vehicles like the DF-17 and America's Dark Eagle - so the only way to justify the space option is to write a genuinely global deny-sanctuary requirement that long range alone cannot satisfy.
causal🗣 Perunconviction: highRS-24DF-21DF-17Dark Eaglekinetic bombardment
So what: Expanding hypersonic and ballistic inventories keep crowding out orbital strike on cost per effect - a reason to discount space-strike program hype.
Perun identifies collapsing launch costs as the one variable that genuinely improved the orbital strike case since 2003, citing a recent US contract for another 18 Falcon 9 launches over 14 months at about 1.6 billion US dollars, while cautioning those figures do not transfer to lifting classified or oversized payloads.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: mediumFalcon 9low Earth orbitUnited Stateskinetic bombardment
So what: Commercial launch pricing is the key input that makes previously absurd space architectures financially arguable - a structural tailwind for launch providers.
Perun argues an orbital rod system's real return may be psychological rather than military, noting many weapons across history commanded more public fear than their raw capabilities merited, so marginal spend might be better placed on media presence than on more satellites and rods - while conceding the system would be massively vulnerable to anti-satellite launchers and would push its owner toward first-strike postures.
contrarian🗣 Perunconviction: mediumkinetic bombardmentASATEmutopiadeterrence
So what: Perceived capability can substitute for real capability in deterrence, so headline weapons announcements often read as signalling rather than capability.
“you can name plenty of weapon systems across history that have commanded more public attention, more fear, and more apprehension than their actual raw capabilities merited” ▶ 47:51
Dual-Use Space Programs and Cheap Resilience 5
Perun quantifies lunar base ambition as out of reach for an individual middle power by noting NASA's plan calls for more than 80 launches to the moon and delivery of north of 210 tons of cargo, a scale no single mid-tier nation can realistically match.
data_point🗣 Perunconviction: highNASAArtemismoon
So what: Lunar infrastructure economics force coalition or commercial participation - single-nation lunar programs are not credible at that cargo scale.
Perun's general lesson for well-funded pet projects is to technically deliver exactly what you were told to do while generating as many genuinely useful outcomes as possible on the side, illustrated by building astronaut selection and training, sovereign launch and landing, rovers and habitats under cover of a 'space marine' mandate.
framework🗣 Perunconviction: highspace marinesEmutopiaastronaut program
So what: Dual-use structuring is how exotic political mandates get converted into durable industrial capability, and a lens for reading seemingly absurd defence line items.
“You find a way to technically deliver exactly what you're being told to do while on the side making sure you generate as many actually useful outcomes as possible.” ▶ 52:09
Perun argues the hard part of a military space program is space itself rather than the weapons, noting Cold War studies of lunar infantry combat envisaged very light, simple small arms and the Soviet Almaz space gun was relatively simple, so a weapons mandate can fund expensive general-purpose capability and be dropped cheaply when political priorities change.
thesis🗣 Perunconviction: highAlmazmoonspace marinesSoviet Union
So what: Military space budgets reliably subsidise civilian-usable launch, habitat and crew capability - the classic R&D spillover pattern, now applied to lunar programs.
“it's not so much that weaponizing space is hard, it's just that space is hard” ▶ 53:54
Perun endorses cheap resilience measures suited to an EW-saturated battlefield where signature management matters, specifically burying power and fibre optic cable to prepared defensive positions in a pre-war period to sustain infantry and guarantee connectivity for forward UAS and UGVs, plus balloons and zeppelins as cheap airborne sensor and drone-relay platforms.
thesis🗣 Perunconviction: highelectronic warfarefibre opticsUGVUASUkraine
So what: Fibre, buried power and cheap high-altitude platforms are low-cost, high-leverage counter-EW investments already validated in Ukraine.
Perun's honest verdict is that the crowdsourced Emutopian package would be a bad investment and that outsourcing a major procurement to the internet is inadvisable, while noting many submissions received outside Patreon were serious alternative force designs rather than attempts to sabotage the brief.
anecdote🗣 Perunconviction: highEmutopiaPatreondefence procurement
So what: Frames the episode as a deliberate thought experiment - the specific buys are illustrative, while the requirements-writing and opportunity-cost lessons are the transferable content.
“Would it be a good investment? No, but it would be an investment.” ▶ 47:40

EconTalk · Russ Roberts 1 episode(s) · 30 insights

Savage Landscapes (with Cal Flyn) 7/27/2662 min30 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Cal Flyn tells Russ Roberts that 'wilderness' is a cultural construct — historically peopled landscapes now legally and emotionally coded as empty — and walks through the operating consequences: rebounding European bear and wolf populations creating a wildland-urban conflict frontier, trophy-hunting revenue (a lion permit in Mozambique's Niasa reserve exceeding $100,000) functioning as the load-bearing finance for reserves that photographic tourism cannot fund, and Kenya's and Zambia's hunting bans producing land conversion and funding reversals. The through-line is a demand-side thesis: wealthy, comfortable populations manufacture fear and discomfort — rewilding, pilgrimage, volcano-chasing — because they find their own landscapes insufficiently wild.
Wilderness as a peopled construct · 4European predator rebound and the wildland-urban interface · 8Trophy-hunting economics and the conservation funding gap · 11The sublime, risk perception and exclusion zones · 3Comfort-anxiety as a recurring cultural cycle · 2Fieldwork as an information edge · 2
Wilderness as a peopled construct 4
Cal Flyn argues that historic concepts of wilderness were traditionally peopled, and that the modern redefinition of wilderness as a place without people is both recent and actively harmful to land management.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highCal FlynThe Savage Landscapeconservationland management
So what: Any conservation designation, carbon-offset, or protected-area investment premised on 'empty land' carries unpriced legal and reputational risk from existing inhabitants.
“historic concepts of wilderness were actually traditionally peopleled. It's only relatively recently that we've come to see wilderness as being a non-people place. And that's caused its own problems.” ▶ 2:41
Flyn's Australian frontier research found that the 'country park' landscape British colonists praised on landing in New South Wales was the engineered product of Aboriginal fire-stick and forest farming, not natural abundance — a pattern she says recurs worldwide.
causal🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highAustraliaNew South WalesAboriginal AustraliansThicker Than Water
So what: Historical 'baseline' ecosystems used to justify restoration targets are often themselves managed landscapes; restoration benchmarks may be mis-specified.
“really what that landscape is is the result of forestic farming. It's the result of the planting of fruit trees. It's the result of tending.” ▶ 35:14
Flyn proposes a human-impact spectrum — tarmac car park at one end, mobile forest peoples' jungle at the other — replacing the binary of nature versus civilization, and notes the 'wild' end is still someone's owned homeland under systems Western legal frameworks struggle to recognize.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highland ownershipindigenous land rightsconservation designations
So what: Land-title ambiguity in nominally wild regions is the recurring failure mode in resource, conservation and offset deals; screen for customary tenure, not just registered title.
“if we understand maybe a tarmac car park at up one end of the spectrum and then I don't know perhaps a a a jungle with um forest peoples who move around a lot at the other end.” ▶ 36:37
Flyn identifies a structural paradox in wilderness demand: the most famous wilderness and pilgrimage sites — Mount Sinai's carved steps now supplemented by a commercial camel track to the summit — become crowded tourist infrastructure that erodes the very solitude sought.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highMount Sinaipilgrimage tourismAntarctica
So what: Congestion is endogenous to wilderness branding — destination assets sold on solitude structurally degrade their own product as volume scales.
“these really well-known wilderness sites become tourist sites or sites of pilgrimage or you know crowds end up coming.” ▶ 17:28
European predator rebound and the wildland-urban interface 8
Romania's brown bear population surge traces to Ceausescu protecting bears decades earlier than the rest of Europe because he personally liked hunting them, and rising Romanian wealth is now pushing new Transylvanian housing into recently forested land, producing bears raiding school canteens and churches.
causal🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highRomaniaTransylvaniaNicolae Ceausescubrown bears
So what: Development-driven wildland-urban interface conflict is a live political risk to European conservation rules and to exurban property expansion in emerging EU markets.
“during the communist regime, Chichescu protected the bears a lot earlier than they were protected elsewhere because he loved to hunt them. And so the numbers have been jumping up and up” ▶ 23:56
Germany, Luxembourg and Belgium are seeing wolves for the first time in over 100 years — in some cases over 200 — as protected predator populations expand across continental Europe.
data_point🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highGermanyLuxembourgBelgiumwolvesEurope
So what: A measurable, continent-wide trend that will force livestock protection spending and revisions to EU species-protection directives.
Predator return imposes real re-skilling and capital costs on European agriculture: Flyn says parts of Iberia are relearning the use of livestock guardian dogs and installing specialist electric fencing after centuries without predators.
data_point🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highIberiaSpainsheep farminglivestock guardian dogselectric fencing
So what: Creates a small but growing addressable market in predator-proofing equipment and compensation schemes, and a cost line for European pastoral agriculture.
“in regions like in parts of Iberia, people are having to relearn old skills of having these shepherd dogs to protect their herds. um to bring in specialist electrical fencing” ▶ 25:38
The 'landscape of fear' model holds that predators reshape vegetation indirectly by keeping herbivores moving, meaning predator populations alter the physical landscape itself through knock-on effects rather than only through kills.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: mediumlandscape of fearapex predatorsgrazersvegetation
So what: A reusable causal model for second-order ecosystem effects; Flyn flags it as 'slightly simplified', so treat trophic-cascade claims in rewilding pitches with caution.
“They have a term called the landscape of fear. Um it's it's a slightly simplified model, but um the idea is that these predators keep um the herbivores and the grazers on the move and that shapes the vegetation that grows.” ▶ 26:21
Russ Roberts describes the US case in reverse: removing wolves from Yellowstone in the early 20th century let elk populations flourish and strip riparian vegetation, degrading willow stands and beaver habitat.
causal🗣 Russ Robertsconviction: highYellowstonewolveselkbeavers
So what: The canonical worked example of trophic cascade used to justify reintroduction budgets on both continents.
“the elimination of wolves in the earlier part of the 20th century is it it allowed the elk to flourish uh which desoiled the uh riberine ecosystems of the park, the willows and the beaver habitat”
Flyn calls UK predator reintroduction economically and psychologically self-sabotaging on utilitarian grounds, yet notes the pro-reintroduction movement is large, vocal and mainstream — evidence that the driver is symbolic rather than economic.
contrarian🗣 Cal Flynconviction: mediumUnited KingdomScotlandrewildingsheep farming
So what: Rewilding policy in Britain will be decided by cultural demand, not cost-benefit, so cost arguments are weak predictors of the outcome for affected landowners.
“if you think about it from a sort of utilitarian point of view it's really an act of self-sabotage to bring these animals back” ▶ 28:24
Because Britain is an island, predators cannot recolonize passively as they are doing across central Europe, so reintroduction there is an explicit, irreversible political decision rather than a fait accompli.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highUnited Kingdomwolvescentral Europerewilding
So what: UK rewilding is a discrete policy event with a datable decision point, unlike the continent's gradual drift — a cleaner catalyst for affected rural land values.
“we're an island. They're not just going to flood back in the way that they're flooding through, you know, most of central Europe at the moment.” ▶ 29:12
Flyn's read of rewilding demand is that people are deliberately importing fear into landscapes they find insufficiently frightening, accepting the artificiality of the result.
thesis🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highrewildingUnited Kingdompredators
So what: Frames rewilding as consumption of experience rather than ecology — implies willingness to pay is more durable than the scientific case.
“They they don't find it to be fearful enough. And so they're trying to bring in fearful elements to kind of return that sense of the wild, however artificial that might be.” ▶ 29:34
Trophy-hunting economics and the conservation funding gap 11
Flyn reports that a lion hunt in Mozambique's Niasa Special Reserve — a reserve roughly the size of Switzerland, divided into hunting blocks on the Tanzanian border — costs in excess of $100,000, with part of that revenue funding anti-poaching patrols and local communities.
data_point🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highNiasaMozambiqueTanzanialion huntinganti-poaching
So what: Concrete unit economics for the wildlife-permit market: a single high-value permit funds enforcement that per-head photographic tourism cannot in unstable regions.
“if you want to go lion hunting, for example, will probably cost you in excess of $100,000.” ▶ 46:24
Security risk is why photographic tourism cannot substitute for hunting revenue in Niasa: shortly after Flyn's visit, a neighboring camp was raided and staff beheaded by Islamists operating in the surrounding miombo woodland.
causal🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highNiasaMozambiqueIslamist insurgencysafari tourism
So what: Explains the persistence of low-volume/high-fee models in insecure geographies — mass-tourism conservation finance is not deployable where insurgency risk exists.
“shortly after we were in Niasa, a neighboring camp was raided and staff members beheaded um, by Islamists hiding in hiding in the sort of Mumbo woodland around there.” ▶ 48:09
Zambia banned trophy hunting and then reversed course because it could not replace the lost conservation finance from other sources — a natural experiment Flyn cites as evidence the revenue is load-bearing.
data_point🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highZambiatrophy hunting banconservation finance
So what: Precedent for reversal risk in any jurisdiction banning consumptive wildlife use without an identified replacement revenue stream.
“have tried to ban trophy hunting and then they've had to go back because they haven't been able to find the finances from other sources.” ▶ 48:33
Kenya's hunting ban since the 1970s coincided with rangeland being converted to economically productive farmland, and Flyn argues wildlife numbers crashed largely because of that land-use conversion rather than hunting pressure.
causal🗣 Cal Flynconviction: mediumKenyahunting banrangelandfarmland conversion
So what: Core mechanism: if wildlife has no revenue, land flips to its next-best economic use — the binding constraint is land opportunity cost, not poaching.
“what has happened in a lot of those places is that what was once rangeand has been converted to economically active farmland. And so the numbers have crashed, not necessarily because they've been hunted out, but for other reasons.” ▶ 55:23
Flyn warns of a coming US conservation funding crisis because hunters and anglers — whose permit purchases underwrite conservation budgets — are aging out and are not being replaced by younger buyers.
prediction🗣 Cal Flynconviction: mediumUnited Stateshunting permitsfishing licensesconservation funding
So what: A demographic funding cliff for US state wildlife agencies — watch for new tax or user-fee mechanisms aimed at non-consumptive outdoor recreation.
“people who are into hunting, fishing, and so on are aging out. And so the younger generation, if they're not going to be buying permits in the same number, that's going to cause a funding crisis um for conservation.” ▶ 48:46
Flyn concludes that the strongest objection to African trophy hunting is not animal ethics but the racial and colonial structure of the model — near-invariably wealthy white, now mainly American, clients — and that this is why many black Africans reject it.
contrarian🗣 Cal Flynconviction: hightrophy huntingAfricaAmerican hunterscolonialism
So what: Reframes the political risk to hunting-based conservation as a legitimacy problem, meaning better welfare or biology data will not defuse opposition.
“it's not so much around the ethics around shooting animals themselves, but it's more about this feeling that it's continuing or preserving colonial ways of interacting um between different groups of people.” ▶ 49:32
Under the hunting-reserve model, residents living on and inside reserves like Niasa are typically barred from hunting themselves and negotiate limited-use deals with landowners or managers — exclusion of locals is part of what is being sold.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highNiasahunting reserveslocal communitiesland rights
So what: Identifies the extractive core of the model and the most likely fault line for future expropriation or renegotiation of concessions.
“the residents of the hunting reserve are not able to hunt themselves and I guess that's what's what's being rented out” ▶ 52:44
Flyn says Namibia's community-run trophy hunting is a happier model than landowner-run concessions because the community itself does the renting — locating the difference in who holds the property right, not in the activity.
thesis🗣 Cal Flynconviction: mediumNamibiacommunity conservanciestrophy huntingproperty rights
So what: Ownership structure, not the presence of hunting, predicts local acceptance and durability — the screen to apply to conservation concession deals.
“in Namibia, it's a lot more communityrun. And so it tends to be a happier model because it's the community themselves renting out rather than a land owner” ▶ 52:54
Roberts frames wildlife decline as a pure property-rights problem: unowned wildlife gives no one an incentive to protect it, so granting a poor village saleable hunting permits creates both an income stream and an incentive to stop uncompensated poaching.
framework🗣 Russ Robertsconviction: highelephantsproperty rightspoachinghunting permits
So what: The canonical incentive argument for consumptive-use conservation; the transferable test is whether a local party captures the cash flow from the asset surviving.
“the one of the biggest problems with wildlife is, ironically for some people is that it's not owned. So, people don't have an incentive to take care of it and protect it.” ▶ 43:42
Flyn pushes back on the village-ownership model by noting a village is not a single decision-making entity — internal views diverge sharply — and that the model imports an understanding of economics that is not traditional locally, requiring costly alignment.
contrarian🗣 Cal Flynconviction: mediumcommunity conservanciesvillage governanceproperty rights
So what: Warns that 'community ownership' in conservation finance hides governance and distribution problems that determine whether the scheme actually holds.
“it's pretty complicated to to have I don't know to speak to the village as an single entity.” ▶ 54:37
Roberts extends the renting-access frame beyond safari: parts of Europe function as a giant museum with weak underlying economies, renting foreigners the right to admire cobblestoned streets and landscapes and crowding out the locals who once used those cafes.
framework🗣 Russ Robertsconviction: mediumEuropetourismovertourism
So what: Same economic structure as hunting concessions — selling access to a scarce experiential asset — which is why overtourism triggers the same backlash dynamics.
“parts of Europe are a giant museum and there's not a great thriving economy in some of the countries that that have this museumlike quality and so you rent essentially the opportunity for people to admire your landscape” ▶ 50:22
The sublime, risk perception and exclusion zones 3
Flyn treats Edmund Burke's 18th-century claim that the sublime is the most powerful emotion known to man as an empirically testable proposition and deliberately chased waterfalls and then Icelandic volcanic eruptions to induce it.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highEdmund Burkethe sublimeIcelandvolcanic eruptions
So what: A model of intensity as a scarce good people will travel and risk their lives to obtain — the demand curve underlying adventure and extreme tourism.
“Edmund Burke uh in the 18th century was talking about how this is the most powerful emotion known to man.” ▶ 38:36
Flyn drove to the edge of a temporary Icelandic exclusion zone around an eruption deemed unsafe for guided access, hiked in alone until she felt unsafe, and describes overshooting the sublime into terror for her life — the most frightened she was in the book.
anecdote🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highIcelandvolcanic eruptionexclusion zonetourist board
So what: Shows how quickly official closure fails to deter determined visitors — a liability and enforcement problem for volcano and disaster tourism sites.
“I think I I just blasted straight through sublime experience and and came out the other side in this like horror terror for my life.” ▶ 41:24
Flyn identifies prohibition itself as an emotional intensifier — drawing on her earlier work on Chernobyl and other fenced-off abandoned places in Islands of Abandonment — arguing that lacking permission to be somewhere heightens the experience.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highChernobylIslands of Abandonmentexclusion zones
So what: Restriction can raise rather than suppress demand for a site; access bans on hazard zones may perversely increase visitation.
“I'm really fascinated about what happens when you feel like you don't have permission to be somewhere that it's an intensifier. You know, your emotions are really on high alert.” ▶ 40:50
Comfort-anxiety as a recurring cultural cycle 2
Flyn argues the wilderness impulse is a periodic reaction to material comfort, citing early-20th-century anti-modernist writing that framed life as unreal amid new electricity and anesthetics in exactly the terms now used about phones and the internet.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highanti-modernismsmartphonesthe internetelectricityanesthetics
So what: Predicts recurring, durable consumer demand for discomfort products — wilderness travel, digital detox, austerity experiences — as a counter-cycle to any comfort technology wave.
“there was a lot of writing around that time that people were somehow not real, that life was somehow not real in the way that we talk about the internet now that we're living through our phones that it's not real” ▶ 59:06
Flyn calls contemporary anxiety about modern life 'old wine in new bottles,' with the search for wilderness resurfacing across eras in culturally specific forms — early medieval Irish monks setting out to sea in coracles, 18th-century Russian hermits seeking forest solitude.
thesis🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highIrelandRussiahermitswilderness
So what: Treats today's tech-driven alienation discourse as a recurring pattern rather than a novel condition — a discount on 'this time is different' narratives about screens.
“old wine in new bottles that this like searching for the wilderness bubbles up again and again. And it's because we're scared of being too comfortable and we do want to have this confrontation with the other” ▶ 1:00:06
Fieldwork as an information edge 2
Flyn's reporting rule, carried from news journalism, is that speaking to people on the ground reliably contradicts the prior you formed from research — and she says she is frequently wrong on arrival.
framework🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highfield reportingresearch methodology
So what: A direct argument for primary channel checks over desk research in any geography where the remote-source picture is thin.
“if you actually speak to people on the ground, they will tell you something slightly different to what you were expecting.” ▶ 32:29
For the regions Flyn covered — Dolpo in Nepal, Yanomami territory, the Batwa in Uganda — the English-language literature is small and often unreliable because it rests on a handful of English-speaking writers, unlike the well-documented Antarctic.
data_point🗣 Cal Flynconviction: highNepalDolpoYanomamiBatwaUgandaAntarctica
So what: Source concentration is the hidden risk in remote-geography research: a few authors can propagate a single, wrong consensus.
“the English language literature is relatively small and is often a little bit unreliable. It's based on a small number of English English speaking writers.”

Jordi Visser 1 episode(s) · 28 insights

The AI Crash Is Over. Here's What Comes Next51 min28 insights2026-08-09
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jordi Visser calls the AI factor 'speed crash' over and the bottom in, citing a ~10% four-day Nasdaq rally, collapsing factor volatility, new all-time highs in IWM, the NYSE Composite and the equal-weight S&P, and ~50% earnings growth with no credit or index-vol contagion. He argues index puts no longer hedge an AI book because factor vol has structurally decoupled from index vol, calls peak gross leverage, and pairs that with a debasement thesis built on the first coordinated US-Japan yen intervention since 1998, a collapsing Truflation reading, and the weakest six-month aggregate payrolls since 2012 ex-COVID.
