Speed-Crash Bottom Call 4
Jordi Visser declares the AI-factor 'speed crash' finished and the bottom in on a probability basis, saying he would be surprised if the lows are taken out because investors are far too bearish relative to the earnings growth that is coming.
thesis🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highS&P 500NasdaqAI trade
So what: Frames the AI-factor washout as a buyable cleansing event rather than the start of a bear market, with the prior lows as the risk-management line.
“The speed crash has occurred, it's over. The bottom is in as far as I'm concerned on a probability basis. If we take out the lows, I'd be surprised, uh mainly because I continue to believe people are way too bearish relative to the reality” ▶ 14:27
The four-day rally in the Nasdaq reached close to 10%, clustering with famous bottoms like Liberation Day, COVID and 2022, and the S&P's breakout from a three-month May-to-July pennant was 6% bigger than the entire prior range - a move Visser says is not something to fade, distinguished from the 2022 bear-market rally by price sitting above an upward-sloping 200-day moving average in 80%-plus of AI thematic names.
data_point🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highQQQNasdaqS&P 500AI thematic portfolio
So what: Thrusts above an upward-sloping 200-day historically mark durable bottoms; a breakout larger than the consolidation it came from favors continuation over reversal.
“And guess what? The rally we had was 6% bigger than the entire range that we saw for that time period. That is not something to fade.” ▶ 4:37
Breadth confirms a bull market rather than a narrow AI melt-up: every S&P sector is up on the year with the index +13%, IWM, the NYSE Composite and the equal-weight S&P all made new all-time highs in the same week, the DAX and FTSE broke out to all-time highs, and the KOSPI 200 is up 61% year-to-date with its technology sector up 116% after the collapse.
data_point🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highS&P 500IWMNYSE CompositeDAXFTSEKOSPI 200SK Hynix
So what: Global, broad-based new highs undercut the contagion case and support staying long beyond mega-cap US tech.
Despite factor volatility hitting 45-year highs on some measures, no contagion reached index or credit markets - the VIX is at its lowest since January, single-name vol has mean-reverted to pre-episode levels and the CDX index behaved just like the VIX - so investors who bought S&P puts lost on both direction and volatility, while the six-day collapse in tech-momentum realized vol shows funds are now hedged or de-grossed and vol-targeted funds and burned pods cannot re-lever into a low-volume summer rally.
causal🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highVIXCDXS&P 500multi-strategy podsvol-target funds
So what: Confirms a leverage/factor event rather than a solvency event, and sets up chase risk as underexposed managers face seasonality fears with no ability to re-lever.
Hedging AI With Factor Vol, Rate-of-Change and Breadth 4
Visser argues factor volatility has structurally decoupled from S&P index volatility because it embeds long-short gross leverage, so index puts no longer hedge an AI thematic book the way they used to.
framework🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highS&P 500factor volatilityindex putslong-short funds
So what: Reprices the hedging stack toward single-name, factor and dispersion hedges instead of cheap-looking index puts for AI-heavy portfolios.
“using index puts is not going to work the way that it used to” ▶ 16:26
Visser explicitly calls peak gross leverage - predicting long-short gross comes down over the next few years as AI compresses time, increases crowding and lowers factor Sharpe ratios - and expects factor vol, measured as an equal weight of momentum, value, quality and beta, to stay structurally elevated in a 25-30 range rather than returning to prior lows.
prediction🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highhedge fundsgross leveragemomentum factorvalue factorquality factor
So what: A multi-year de-grossing regime means more frequent violent factor unwinds, higher hedging costs and structurally lower returns for crowded quant and pod strategies.
“I'm making the call that we are at peak gross leverage and that we will see leverage come down over the course of the next few years” ▶ 15:48
Visser's replacement risk model combines a 50-day rate of change on his 100-name global AI thematic index with internal breadth: a roughly 50% move over 50 trading days guarantees a fall, risk starts growing above 20%, and the warning fires only when elevated rate of change coincides with breadth breaking down inside the 100 names - a system he built because the 252-day S&P rate-of-change recession model that flagged September 2007 is too slow in an exponential-innovation cycle.
framework🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highAI thematic portfolioMorgan StanleyS&P 500
So what: A concrete, replicable de-risking trigger for AI exposure that fired ahead of the July momentum break, when traditional macro lead indicators would have signaled too late.
Universal AI access to backtesting and portfolio optimization increases crowding and produces more bubbles, parabolas and speed crashes, compressing market time as well as economic time - Visser notes July was the worst month for momentum in his entire dataset - which makes terminal values harder to estimate and lets a company re-rate from a 30 PE to a 20 PE very quickly, as he says is happening to Google now.
causal🗣 Jordi Visserconviction: highGooglemomentum factorGavin Bakerquant strategies
So what: Multiple compression is now a fast discrete event rather than a slow grind, so hedging cadence and rebalancing frequency must rise permanently.
“AI compresses economic time, but it it may also compress market time. The month of July for momentum was worse than any year over the data that I have.” ▶ 27:32
AI Demand Data, Compute Shortage and the Memory Bottleneck 6
Per Gavin Baker, the AI sell-off is disconnected from the quantitative data: he has found no real negative demand metric and not a single instance of deceleration, with GPU availability, GPU rental pricing and the spot price of DRAM all accelerating.
data_point🗣 Jordi Visser, citing Gavin Bakerconviction: highGavin BakerNvidiaGPUsDRAM

GPU rental economics are inflating rather than deflating: a B200 cluster that rented around mid-$2 per hour seven months ago is now expected to rent for just under $4 per GPU hour, undercutting the thesis that AI chips have short useful lives and will not survive their depreciation schedules.
data_point🗣 Jordi Visser, citing Gavin Bakerconviction: highNvidiaB200GPU rental market

Ethereum’s New EIP Could Break DeFi54 min28 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Bankless host David hosts Stonnie (Aave) and Mike (EtherFi) to attack a new Ethereum 'stake targeting / stake tapering' EIP from six Ethereum Foundation researchers that would drive staking issuance toward zero as staked ETH approaches ~50% (currently ~34% staked). The guests argue it kills solo-staker economics at a ~2% reserve yield, centralizes staking into zero-cost-of-capital exchanges and DATs plus one dominant LST, triggers a capital exodus from seven of the top ten DeFi protocols, and turns ETH into a yen-style carry-trade funding leg that rotates capital into stablecoins and competing chains. Both hosts and guests also flag the process legitimacy problem: the EIP landed days before the hard-fork inclusion meeting, and the host closes predicting it will not pass.
Stake Tapering EIP Mechanics · 4Solo Staker Economics and Validator Centralization · 5DeFi Blast Radius · 5Nominal vs Real Yield and the Fixed-Supply Argument · 5ETH as Funding Leg and Rotation into Stablecoins · 3Institutional Demand and DAT Underwriting · 3Process Legitimacy and Ossification Risk · 3
Stake Tapering EIP Mechanics 4
Six Ethereum Foundation researchers and developers proposed a 'stake targeting / stake tapering' EIP because there is currently no cap on the incentive to stake ETH — roughly 34% of all ETH is staked today and the proposers fear it marches toward 100%, with vanilla ETH progressively replaced by staked-ETH derivatives from Lido, Rocketpool and EtherFi.
data_point🗣 David (Bankless host)conviction: highEthereumETHEFLidoRocketpoolEtherfi
So what: A live protocol-level change to ETH monetary policy is the single biggest swing factor for LST issuers and ETH staking-yield-linked assets; watch the hard-fork inclusion process.
“Right now there is about 34% of all ETH is staked and the worry that this EIP has is that that will slowly march increasingly close to 100% because there's no reason for it not to.” ▶ 0:54
The EIP's mechanic is a taper in which staking yield approaches zero as staked ETH approaches a 50% target, burning the issuance above that point rather than paying it to validators.
framework🗣 David (Bankless host)conviction: highEthereumETHstaking yield
So what: Any model of ETH staking revenue, LST fee income or DAT cash flows must stress-test a scenario where the marginal staking yield asymptotes to zero above a 50% stake ratio.
“essentially yield approaches zero as staked teeth approaches 50%” ▶ 15:08
The proposers' security argument is that Ethereum is overpaying for security and that roughly 30–40% of ETH staked already provides ample security, so no more than 50% is needed.
thesis🗣 David (Bankless host)conviction: mediumEthereumETHEF
So what: The whole EIP rests on an unproven 'sufficient security' threshold; if that assumption is contested successfully, the proposal loses its core justification.
“something around 30 to 40% of ETH staked to Ethereum provides ample security according to the opinions of these researchers” ▶ 1:57
The EIP was introduced only days before the All Core Devs meeting where proposals were considered for inclusion in the next hard fork, creating the impression it was being squeezed in without debate — which backfired and produced near-universal opposition outside the EF.
causal🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highEthereumEFhard fork
So what: Process optics, not economics, may be what kills this EIP — a template for how contentious Ethereum monetary changes get defeated.
“it was proposed with you know a matter of days uh leading up to the meeting where where these proposals were discussed for inclusion and in the next hard fork” ▶ 8:05
Solo Staker Economics and Validator Centralization 5
Solo stakers have the highest cost basis on Ethereum because they get no economies of scale, and at current costs a 2% to 2.5% yield is roughly break-even, so any issuance cut pushes every solo staker into a loss.
data_point🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highEthereumsolo stakersnode operators
So what: The floor under Ethereum's decentralized validator set is a hard cost number, not ideology — issuance cuts translate mechanically into node shutdowns.
“a two and a half% yield, 2% yield is is pretty much break even at this point um in terms of the cost of operating a note” ▶ 9:08
An ETHStaker survey found the reserve price for most solo stakers is 2% — below that yield they say they will turn off their nodes — making the belief that thousands of operators will keep running at a loss 'magical thinking'.
data_point🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highETH stakersolo stakersEthereum
So what: 2% staking yield is the empirically stated cliff for solo-staker attrition; it is the level to watch for validator-set decentralization metrics.
“there was a survey fairly recently by done by ETH staker uh that showed that the sort of reserve price uh for most solar stakers is 2%” ▶ 9:45
Because exchanges and treasury companies like Coinbase, Binance, Sharplink and Bitmine have effectively no cost of capital on user-deposited or balance-sheet ETH, they would keep staking even at 20 basis points, so the taper causes an exodus of independent operators and centralizes the network in exactly the entities it claims to protect against.
causal🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highCoinbaseBinanceSharplinkBitmineEthereumsolo stakers
So what: Post-taper, staking market share would concentrate in listed custodians and DATs — bullish their staking-share narrative, bearish Ethereum's decentralization story.
“this is going to centralize the network because entities like Coinbase and Binance and Sharlink and you know Bitmine they have no cost of capital right” ▶ 10:09
At 20 to 50 basis points of staking yield an LST issuer earns only about one basis point of revenue, so liquid staking would immediately consolidate into a single giant LST because only massive scale could fund developers and audits.
causal🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highLSTsLidoEtherfiRocketpool
So what: The taper is a winner-take-all forcing function in liquid staking — smaller LST tokens would be structurally impaired while the largest incumbent consolidates share.
“LSTs will immediately concentrate into one giant LST, liquid staking tokens, because at 20 basis points, 50 basis points, you're only making one basis point of of revenue” ▶ 10:34
Beyond lower cash flows, the tapering design creates unpredictable tax consequences for solo stakers, who could receive a tax bill on staking income large enough to push their net position negative.
causal🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: mediumsolo stakersEthereumtaxes
So what: Tax drag on a variable, tapering yield is a second-order reason retail staking participation would fall faster than the raw yield math implies.
“creates also uh very unpredictable tax consequences. So you know and the question is then if someone gets a $500 bill for staking uh they can even go into negative as well” ▶ 5:00
DeFi Blast Radius 5
If the stake tapering EIP passes, seven of the top ten DeFi protocols would face a massive capital exodus as the ETH-denominated base yield they are built on disappears.
prediction🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highDeFiEthereumlending protocols
So what: The concentration of DeFi TVL in staking-yield-dependent strategies means the largest ETH DeFi tokens would be the direct casualties of this EIP.
“the seven of the top 10 DeFi protocols would face a massive capital exodus” ▶ 15:16
Staking yield functions as Ethereum's foundational yield layer the way short-term T-bills do in traditional finance, with roughly a hundred layers of derivatives stacked on top — so changing the issuance curve is analogous to the US government tapering T-bill rates when too many people buy them.
framework🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highT billsEthereumDeFiUS government
So what: A reusable mental model for pricing protocol-level yield changes: the risk is not the base rate itself but the leveraged stack built on its predictability.
“if you're messing with this foundational yield layer on top of which a lot of other things are stacked I mean you're going to you're going to really break the system” ▶ 16:13
Most DeFi yield on Ethereum ultimately derives from native staking routed through liquid staking derivatives, which create the borrowing demand in lending protocols by letting users enter and exit staking without going through the validator queue — so 'go find yield in DeFi instead' is circular reasoning.
causal🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: highDeFiliquid staking derivativeslending protocolsEthereum
So what: Lending-protocol borrow demand and looping strategies are downstream of staking yield; a taper compresses lending revenue across the sector, not just LST fees.
“a lot of the yield actually comes from these u uh uh liquid staking um uh derivatives that basically just makes it more feasible to stake and come in and out and then that creates the the borrowing demand” ▶ 18:29
Removing the staking base yield forces ETH holders further up the risk curve because there is no other scalable, low-risk source of ETH-denominated yield outside native staking.
thesis🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: highETHDeFistaking
So what: Post-taper, ETH yield products would have to take real credit or duration risk, raising systemic fragility in DeFi rather than reducing it.
“I don't particularly know many yield sources at a scalable way where you can actually get yield on ETH beyond the sort of like a uh kind of like a risk-free or this low uh risk um yield that is derived from uh native uh uh staking” ▶ 18:59
Mike argues liquid staking tokens deliver application-layer user protections that would be inappropriate at the network level — for example governance-approved forks to rescue funds after a North Korean exploit — for only five to ten basis points, and that the better fix for the EF's concerns is to enshrine LSTs in the protocol rather than alter the issuance curve.
thesis🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highLSTsEtherfiLidoNorth KoreaEthereum
So what: An enshrined-LST design is the concrete alternative on the table; it would disintermediate Lido and EtherFi's staking business while preserving DeFi's yield base.
“My perspective is and I've argued this in the past is that we should just enshrine LSTs and that that kicks” ▶ 14:12
Nominal vs Real Yield and the Fixed-Supply Argument 5
Mike argues a fixed money supply is bad economics for a real economy — citing Milton Friedman's prescription of a fixed 3% annual money-supply growth — and calls the push toward zero ETH issuance a 'bitcoin brainworm' because deflation is in some ways worse than inflation.
contrarian🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highMilton FriedmanBitcoinETHmoney supply
So what: The strongest intellectual counter to ultrasound-money framing: a deflationary ETH becomes hard to use as debt collateral or money, capping Ethereum's economic ambitions.
“this argument that we need to have zero issuance in some at some point is a really bad argument that it's like a bitcoin brainworm” ▶ 13:43
Mike contends the case is stronger that ETH staking yield should be higher, not lower, since even at 100% staked and zero burn the inflation is about 1.5% — and with EIP1559 burn it is lower — a trivial problem relative to breaking the yield layer.
contrarian🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highETHEIP1559staking yield
So what: Frames the whole debate as optimizing away ~1.5% of dilution at the cost of the ecosystem's core yield infrastructure — a poor risk/reward for ETH holders.
“let's say burn goes to zero 100% of ETH is staked how much of a problem is 1.5% % yield. I think the case is actually stronger that the yield should be higher than that it should be lower.” ▶ 16:39
Because in traditional markets the derivatives market is more than ten times the size of the spot market, Mike argues that if Ethereum becomes the platform for all of finance essentially all ETH will end up in derivatives regardless of issuance policy, making the EIP's 'preserve vanilla ETH' goal a red herring.
framework🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highderivativesEthereumETHLSTs
So what: If derivatization is inevitable, the investable layer is the issuers of ETH derivatives and their fee capture, not vanilla ETH holding.
“If you look at the trady markets the market for derivatives is 10 times more more than 10 times the size of the market for spot.” ▶ 12:06
The host's steelman is that ETH staking yield is purely nominal — newly minted ETH paid to stakers — while real yield is only transaction fees and burn, so tapering removes a fake game and lets users hold vanilla, intermediary-free ETH without being inflated away.
framework🗣 David (Bankless host)conviction: mediumETHstaking yieldEthereumburn
So what: The nominal-vs-real-yield distinction is the cleanest bull case for the EIP and the argument LST holders must be able to rebut.
“the ETH stake rate is a nominal yield because we are minting ETH as we are giving it to the ETH to the ETH stakers. Real yield is not happening. Real yield is the transaction fees that are paid to stakers” ▶ 26:34
Mike's rebuttal is that staking de facto serves two different functions — paying for network security and providing an economy's savings rate — and that if the economy grows faster than issuance, stakers earn genuine real yield; conflating the two is what generates the confusion behind the EIP.
framework🗣 Mike (EtherFi)conviction: highEthereumstakingETH
So what: Separating 'security budget' from 'savings rate' is the analytical frame for judging any future Ethereum issuance proposal.
“staking uh is de facto serving these two purposes that on the one hand it's providing security to the Ethereum network and there's lots of complications around that and then on the other hand it also provides this again de facto benefit” ▶ 33:58
ETH as Funding Leg and Rotation into Stablecoins 3
Stonnie argues that stripping ETH of its yield turns it into a funding leg like the Japanese yen in the world's largest carry trade — borrowed cheaply and sold to buy a productive asset — creating structural sell pressure on ETH, the opposite of the proposal's stated goal of making ETH better money.
causal🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: highETHJapanese yencarry tradeDeFi
So what: A zero-yield ETH would likely see rising borrow demand paired with spot selling — a bearish flow dynamic to watch in on-chain lending markets.
“And if there's no yield on uh on it on DeFi, what it means is that um it as an asset becomes a funding leg” ▶ 6:26
Stonnie predicts stablecoins are the biggest winners from an ETH yield taper, as holders who want cash flow rotate out of ETH positions into on-chain stablecoin yields, mirroring how low rates in traditional finance push money between risk assets and cash.
prediction🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: highstable coinsETHDeFi
So what: Trade expression: long stablecoin-yield infrastructure and short ETH-denominated yield products if the EIP gains traction.
“Um and I think there uh the biggest the biggest winners are actually stable coins” ▶ 19:29
Stonnie warns that value leaving ETH would go not only to stablecoins but to competing networks such as Solana, which he calls the worst possible outcome for Ethereum and a return to the earliest, most competitive phase of the blockchain story.
prediction🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: mediumEthereumSalanastable coinsETH
So what: Frames the EIP as a relative-value risk for ETH versus competing L1s, not just an internal Ethereum design question.
“otherwise if that doesn't exist all that value goes into stable coins um and also competing uh networks and that is the worst outcome for Ethereum” ▶ 40:31
Institutional Demand and DAT Underwriting 3
David argues killing staking yield would be devastating for Tom Lee, who deployed roughly $10 billion into ETH and uses the staking yield as his primary tool for selling ETH to other institutions — and worse for every future institutional buyer who would have copied him.
thesis🗣 David (Bankless host)conviction: highTom LeeBitmineSharplinkETHinstitutions
So what: ETH treasury companies are the marginal institutional bid; a yield taper directly undermines their pitch and mNAV narrative.
“imagine what that message would be like if like one year after Tom Lee buys $10 billion of ETH, the ETH protocol kills yield and now he doesn't get any yield” ▶ 41:21
Stonnie says staking yield is what lets ETH treasury companies underwrite ETH as a cash-flow asset, and without it they would be forced into 'ponzenomics' of the kind MicroStrategy runs to sustain their premium.
causal🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: highmicro strategyETHDATsinstitutions
So what: Distinguishes ETH DATs from BTC DATs on the basis of a real cash-flow claim; removing yield collapses that differentiation.
“if they don't have any yield they will have to do you know ponzenomics like what micro strategy is doing” ▶ 41:48
Stonnie cites a Joseph Chalom post from Sharplink to argue the timing is uniquely bad because Ethereum is in the 'last mile' of asset distribution — ETFs, institutional adoption, and a CLARITY Act that would let every US bank launch crypto custody and lending businesses without pre-approval — with ETH's savings rate being the feature that positions it ahead of Bitcoin and Solana.
thesis🗣 Stonnie (Aave)conviction: highJoseph ShalomSharplinkclarity actETFsBitcoinSalanaETH
So what: If the CLARITY Act passes, bank distribution of ETH would hinge on its yield; a taper would remove ETH's key differentiator right as that channel opens.
“if clarity act passes obviously what it means that every single bank uh in in in in US can actually set up uh crypto-based businesses without any sort of a pre-approval”
Process Legitimacy and Ossification Risk 3
Mike argues the perception that this change was jammed through governance destroys Ethereum's credibility with nation states and large institutions — El Salvador chose Bitcoin precisely because it is ossified and predictable — and that this episode creates zero chance a nation state builds on Ethereum.

So what: Reframes monetary-policy tinkering as a direct hit to Ethereum's credibly-neutral moat versus Bitcoin — a long-horizon adoption risk, not a short-term yield question.

Stonnie says the biggest fear in the community is not the EIP itself but that the Ethereum Foundation's researchers are disconnected from DeFi builders and will not listen to concrete feedback, repeating a pattern from earlier proposals that drew backlash.

Stonnie argues Ethereum's real problem is demand-side and product completeness — it still lacks good privacy and good scalability while security is its one finished component — so the ecosystem should stop playing issuance games instead of axing the one thing that works.

Risk Reversal · Dan Nathan 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

AI Is Rewiring Retail Investing with Jim Swartwout & Jay Jacobs45 min25 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Two-part Risk Reversal episode: Guy Adami interviews Jim Swartwout (rendered as 'Jim Swartz' in the auto-transcript), CEO of Prosperum Fintech Holdings and a four-decade brokerage operator across Schwab, E*TRADE, Scottrade and Robinhood, on how AI bots, zero commissions and collapsed payment-for-order-flow economics are rewiring retail brokerage; then Dan Nathan interviews Jay Jacobs, BlackRock's US head of equity ETFs, on IQQQ, SOXX, tokenization, Korea's leveraged-ETF blowup and owning the full AI value chain. The connective thesis is access — fractional shares, low-share-price ETFs and private-market products keep widening the retail funnel while index concentration forces investors to take an explicit view on the Mag 7 and semis.
Retail brokerage economics after zero commissions · 5AI bots as the new full-service broker · 3Retail flow behaviour: rotation, community, and the death of day trading · 4Macro read: valuations, a sub-15 VIX, and the midterm setup · 3ETF proliferation and engineering around index concentration · 5Tokenization, Korea's leveraged-ETF blowup, and the AI value chain · 5
Retail brokerage economics after zero commissions 5
Payment for order flow has collapsed from roughly a nickel per share in the late 1980s to about $0.0001 per share today, which Jim Swartwout argues removes PFOF as a legitimate cost objection to retail trading.
data_point🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highpayment for order flowProsperum Fintech Holdingsretail brokerage
So what: With per-share order-flow economics this compressed, brokerage revenue growth must come from account growth, margin, lending and premium services rather than PFOF rebates.
“payment for order flow was a nickel a share when I started. It's point zero zero zero one now. I mean, it's it's not a problem. It's never been a problem.”
Swartwout says it is effectively impossible to compete on price in retail brokerage now that commissions are zero, so differentiation has moved to bespoke service, brokers with 20-plus years of experience and AI embedded throughout the platform.
thesis🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highMoomooInteractive BrokersRobinhoodAvant GardeProsperum Fintech Holdings
So what: Expect margin pressure and consolidation among undifferentiated fintech brokerage entrants; survivors monetize service layers, not trades.
“it's hard to compete right now at zero commissions. You know, you have to differentiate yourself somehow.” ▶ 16:18
Swartwout treats the move from 24/5 to full 24/7 equity trading as a certainty rather than a possibility, framing it purely as a question of timing.
prediction🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: high24/7 tradingretail brokeragemarket structure
So what: 24/7 equities reshapes overnight market-making, clearing and staffing economics — an infrastructure build-out opportunity and a cost problem for brokers without overnight coverage.
“You can trade 24/5 now at some firms. We're going to 24/7 without a doubt. It's just a matter of time.” ▶ 4:30
Prosperum's Avant Garde broker-dealer launched its retail platform in April with equities, options and ETFs plus a partnership giving accredited investors access to private, pre-IPO names such as SpaceX, with futures and crypto still to come and crypto deliberately deprioritized.
data_point🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highAvant GardeProsperum Fintech HoldingsSpaceXprivate marketscryptofutures
So what: Pre-IPO access is becoming a standard retail brokerage feature rather than a niche, while a brokerage CEO slow-walking crypto is a soft demand signal for retail crypto volumes.
“Crypto's kind of in an odd place right now, so it hasn't been a rush.” ▶ 17:29
Swartwout argues retail options are no longer treated as lottery tickets and that once properly educated, customers recognize options are not inherently riskier than other trades — with Avant Garde building a 10-part on-demand webinar series running from options basics to spreads and straddles.
thesis🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highoptionsAvant GardeTrade Monsterretail investors
So what: Structural growth in retail options literacy supports continued expansion of retail options volume, a key revenue line for brokers and market makers.
“once customers really understand stand options, they understand that they're not any riskier than most other trades.” ▶ 11:01
AI bots as the new full-service broker 3
Prosperum's AI goal is to recreate 1980s full-service broker attentiveness — proactive account reviews, expiring-options alerts, roll suggestions — with no human on the line, with bots explicitly designed to be full-service brokers.
framework🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highAIProsperum Fintech HoldingsAvant Gardeoptions
So what: If AI collapses the cost of advice, the retail brokerage moat shifts from execution price to proactive account intelligence — a direct threat to human advisory and call-center cost structures.
“We want our bots to be full-service brokers, basically. And we're on our way there.” ▶ 9:12
Avant Garde already runs a customer-facing voice bot named Alex that discusses strategies overnight after 5:00 Eastern when human brokers are off-desk, alongside an internal bot answering account-level questions and a Winston AI partnership supplying research and analytics inside the trading app.
data_point🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highAvant GardeWinston AIvoice AIAlex
So what: Overnight voice AI is the cheapest path to supporting 24/7 trading, and brokers buying rather than building research LLMs creates a B2B market for embedded financial-research AI.
“We have a voice bot that can talk to you about any kind of strategy you want or anything else overnight when we don't have brokers there.” ▶ 9:16
Prosperum offers letter-of-direction trading where a newsletter subscriber's recommended trades are executed automatically without the client placing them, and lets third-party trading communities route orders through its APIs.
data_point🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highAvant Gardeletter of direction tradingtrading communitiesAPIs
So what: Newsletter- and community-driven auto-execution converts social sentiment directly into mechanical order flow, amplifying the retail crowding effects discussed elsewhere in the episode.
Retail flow behaviour: rotation, community, and the death of day trading 4
From his six years at Robinhood, Swartwout observed retail flow always concentrating in one or two dominant stocks at a time, with the top name holding for months before flow rotated wholesale into the next one — a pattern he says is visible in daily market volumes and driven by social media discussion.
framework🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highRobinhoodretail flowmarket concentrationsocial mediaReddit
So what: Retail crowding is serial and rotational, not diversified — a mental model for timing single-name gamma, squeeze setups and crowding unwinds.
“retail tends to move in waves and they shift from one to another stock and you see one being the top stock for months and then suddenly the other one takes over.” ▶ 5:36
Swartwout explicitly retires the 'dumb money' framing for retail, arguing that community debate before trades has left retail as well informed as professionals or better in some cases.
contrarian🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highretail investorstrading communitiesReddit
So what: Strategies premised on fading retail as uninformed flow are increasingly mispriced; the remaining edge is in positioning and crowding, not information asymmetry.
“we used to call, you know, pure retail dumb dumb money. I mean, it's not anymore. There's there's no doubt retail has as much information as anyone else, if not more in some cases.” ▶ 7:09
Swartwout says retail behaviour has structurally shifted away from 1990s-style day trading toward weekly dollar-cost averaging into fractional shares and the thousands of available ETFs, which he argues mechanically props the market higher — enabled by AI tools and fractional trading letting a $500 account build the portfolio that once required roughly $10,000.
causal🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highday tradingdollar cost averagingfractional sharesETFsAI
So what: A persistent systematic retail bid explains dip-buying resilience and argues against expecting retail capitulation into drawdowns, while the lower minimum expands the retail base downward in balance size.
“back in the '90s everyone was day trading. People aren't day trading anymore.” ▶ 12:35
Swartwout names prediction markets as the single most surprising area of client demand and openly admits he does not understand why investors use them; he also notes client geographic interest has rotated from European markets last year back into US markets this year.
anecdote🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: mediumprediction marketsUS equitiesEuropean equitiesretail flow
So what: Prediction-market demand is running ahead of even veteran operators' expectations, while momentum-following geographic allocation means US underperformance would trigger an equally abrupt rotation out.
“I think the one that surprised me the most is prediction markets. I don't quite understand why you would do prediction markets, but that's just my personal personal opinion.” ▶ 19:11
Macro read: valuations, a sub-15 VIX, and the midterm setup 3
Swartwout names valuation as his single biggest market concern, drawing the analogy to the 2000 and 2007-2008 reversals, and says that while this year's earnings have been exceptional with very few and only small misses, forward projected earnings must keep pace or current prices become a problem.
thesis🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highS&P 500valuationsearnings2007-2008dot-com
So what: A brokerage CEO flagging valuation rather than liquidity as the risk makes forward estimate revisions, not rates, the variable to watch for a multiple-compression top.
“the forward projected earnings have got to keep up, or else we're going to have an issue with some of these prices.” ▶ 14:14
With the VIX below 15 against historic single-stock volatility, Swartwout concedes the divergence is odd and inexplicable and reports heavy current client demand for portfolio hedges precisely because the setup is hard to explain.
data_point🗣 Jim Swartwout and Guy Adamiconviction: mediumVIXsingle stock volatilityportfolio hedgingoptions
So what: Cheap index vol against expensive single-name vol is a live dispersion setup and makes index hedges cheap relative to realized idiosyncratic risk.
“you have a VIX as we're sitting here today, which is south of 15, which to me is remarkable” ▶ 14:32
Swartwout expects volatility to build into the midterm election as polls emerge, then predicts the market rallies after the election regardless of which side wins because the outcome itself removes uncertainty.
prediction🗣 Jim Swartwout, CEO Prosperum Fintech Holdingsconviction: highmidterm electionsVIXS&P 500
So what: An outcome-agnostic post-election rally call makes the tradable variable the vol crush around the event rather than directional political positioning.
“I think post-election, regardless of the results, the market's going to rally because it'll have more certainty, more clarity.” ▶ 20:52
ETF proliferation and engineering around index concentration 5
An average of six ETFs launched every business day in the US in the first half of this year, and there are now more listed ETFs than single stocks in the United States — against BlackRock's roughly 1,700 ETFs globally.
data_point🗣 Jay Jacobs, US head of equity ETFs at BlackRockconviction: highETFsBlackRockiSharesUS equities
So what: Product supply now exceeds underlying single-name supply, implying brutal fee competition and a coming shakeout of subscale funds.
“In the first half of this year, there's been six ETFs on average launching every business day. And yes, there are more ETFs than single stocks listed in the United States now.” ▶ 33:34
BlackRock launched IQQQ, an iShares Nasdaq 100 ETF, at 12 basis points waived to 10 through July 31, 2027, and deliberately set a low share price so an investor with as little as $25 can buy a whole share and options traders get a cheaper Nasdaq 100 underlying.
data_point🗣 Jay Jacobs, US head of equity ETFs at BlackRockconviction: highIQQQQQQNasdaq 100BlackRockiSharesoptions
So what: A direct fee-and-share-price attack on one of the largest and most profitable ETFs in the market — watch QQQ's fee response and asset migration as the tell.
“It is 12 basis points, way down to 10 basis points through July 31st, 2027.” ▶ 24:06
With the top 10 Nasdaq 100 names — the Mag 7 plus three others — making up nearly 50% of the index weight, BlackRock split the index into QTOP (top 30 names) and QNEXT (next 70) so clients can express an explicit view on concentration rather than accept the index weighting.
framework🗣 Jay Jacobs and Dan Nathanconviction: highQTOPQNEXTNasdaq 100Mag 7IQQQBlackRock
So what: QTOP versus QNEXT is a clean pair-trade instrument for the mega-cap-versus-broadening debate without taking single-stock risk.
“Like it or not, you really need to think about concentration as a function of the markets that we're in today, and it's really about bringing out more tools for investors to be able to express a view on it.” ▶ 27:26
Nathan argues SOXX is better constructed than SMH because SMH's top two holdings, Nvidia and Taiwan Semiconductor, are a little more than 30% of that fund, while Jacobs confirms SOXX caps single-name exposure at around 7 to 8% across US-listed semiconductor companies and ADRs.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathan and Jay Jacobsconviction: highSOXXSMHNvidiaTaiwan Semiconductorsemiconductors
So what: Capped versus uncapped semi ETFs are materially different trades: SMH functions as a leveraged Nvidia/TSMC proxy while SOXX is the diversified sector expression.
Jacobs argues semiconductors have graduated in three years from an interesting cyclical subsector to a foundational component of the economy, such that every investor now needs an explicit view on their semi exposure the way they would for healthcare, financials or energy.
thesis🗣 Jay Jacobs, US head of equity ETFs at BlackRockconviction: highsemiconductorsSOXXAIsector allocation
So what: Reframing semis as a core sector rather than a cyclical trade implies structurally higher baseline allocations and a lower cyclical discount for the group.
“semiconductors went from kind of this interesting cyclical subsector to something that is now essentially a foundational component of our economy.” ▶ 30:15
Tokenization, Korea's leveraged-ETF blowup, and the AI value chain 5
Jacobs frames tokenization as two payoffs — 24/7 global access to US assets and operational cost and friction reduction — but cautions the binding constraint is building a market-making and regulatory environment around it, not the technology.
framework🗣 Jay Jacobs, US head of equity ETFs at BlackRockconviction: hightokenizationBlackRockmarket making24/7 tradingETFs
So what: The world's largest asset manager treats tokenization as an access-and-cost story whose bottleneck is liquidity provision — favoring incumbent market makers over crypto-native rails.
“if you expand assets to trade 24/7, that's one of the first considerations is building liquidity in a market making environment and a regular regulatory environment around it uh to promote market quality.” ▶ 37:00
Korean retail investors have lost more than $35 billion in the recent KOSPI correction, with protests outside fund management companies, after SK Hynix and Samsung grew to a little more than 50% of the index and leveraged and inverse ETFs came to dominate KOSPI-related trading volume.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathanconviction: highKOSPISK HynixSamsungSouth Korealeveraged ETFsmemory
So what: A live case study of concentrated index plus levered retail products unwinding — a template risk for US mega-cap concentration and the single-stock leveraged ETF complex.
“since you had this correction over the last few weeks or so, you know, retail investors have lost more than 35 billion dollars.” ▶ 38:34
BlackRock has deliberately never launched daily inverse or leveraged ETFs, offering delta-one country exposure such as its iShares MSCI South Korea ETF plus options-based income and buffer strategies instead, because levered products fail its three-part investment-rationale, client-need and product-quality filter; Nathan separately calls such products casino-like and says his shows have never advocated them.
contrarian🗣 Jay Jacobs and Dan Nathanconviction: highBlackRockiSharesleveraged ETFsinverse ETFsSouth Korea ETFTaiwan
So what: The largest issuer abstaining from the fastest-growing retail product category is a ceded revenue pool worth watching if regulators tighten — and a signal the flow lacks experienced-trader endorsement.
“But today we have not brought out daily inverse or leveraged ETFs.” ▶ 40:19
Jacobs sizes AI's economic impact at something on the order of $10-15 trillion over the next several years while stressing the theme is still early, and argues the hard problem is pricing something this early in its evolution.
prediction🗣 Jay Jacobs, US head of equity ETFs at BlackRockconviction: highAIBlackRockmega forces
So what: BlackRock's house number for the AI opportunity — the anchor behind its active AI product push and a benchmark for judging whether current AI valuations already discount it.
“this could be, you know, something to the order of 10, 15 trillion dollars of economic impact over the next several years.” ▶ 42:31
Jacobs argues the market has focused too narrowly on GPUs and hyperscalers and maps the fuller AI value chain as power infrastructure, physical data centers and their real estate, the wider semiconductor stack beyond GPUs including CPUs and memory, proprietary-data owners, frontier model developers, and AI-adopting application companies at the top — which is why BlackRock launched BAI, an actively managed AI ETF run by technology portfolio manager Tony Kim.
framework🗣 Jay Jacobs, US head of equity ETFs at BlackRockconviction: highBAITony KimAIGPUshyperscalerspower infrastructuredata centersmemorylarge language models
So what: A concrete checklist for diversifying an AI book out of the crowded GPU/hyperscaler trade into power, real estate, data owners and AI adopters — and BAI's holdings are a readable proxy for institutional AI value-chain positioning.
“the markets have been really narrowly focused on just a couple of names and a couple of different components of the AI ecosystem.” ▶ 43:04

Animal Spirits · Carlson & Batnick 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Is IMAX a Screaming Buy? | LFTC44 min25 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
IMAX CEO Rich Gelfond argues IMAX has become a structural gatekeeper in Hollywood — an IMAX slot is now part of the studio green-light process and filmmakers pitch IMAX before they have a distributor — while the hosts press him on why a brand they rate alongside Disney and Nike still carries only a ~$3B market cap. Gelfond details the untapped monetization levers (merchandise, film prints, mispriced IMAX tickets at AMC, a China automotive AV licensing deal) and the 2027-28 slate backlog that gives IMAX unusual forward visibility.
IMAX inside the studio green-light process · 3The Odyssey: box office proof points and indexing · 5Untapped monetization: merch, ticket pricing, film prints · 4Non-Hollywood content and box-office lumpiness · 3Forward visibility: backlog, guidance and the 2027-28 slate · 3Valuation gap, M&A posture and industry structure · 7
IMAX inside the studio green-light process 3
IMAX has moved upstream from a distribution add-on to part of the green-light decision itself: at most major studios an IMAX release slot is negotiated when a film is approved, alongside studio marketing commitments (Paramount and Tom Cruise both committed spend and promotion for Mission: Impossible 8 right after MI7 finished its run).
thesis🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXParamountTom CruiseMission: Impossible
So what: If IMAX is embedded at green-light, its revenue is contracted years ahead and its negotiating leverage on marketing and date selection rises — a structurally better position than a per-film licensor.
“So the business like you know we're part of the green light process now and I would say at most of the major studios an IMAX release is part of the green light.” ▶ 12:43
IMAX turned down Avengers: Doomsday at Christmas to honor an earlier commitment to Denis Villeneuve's Dune 3, which was shot in part on IMAX cameras and will get the same number of film prints as The Odyssey.
anecdote🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXDune 3Denis VilleneuveAvengers: DoomsdayDisneyMarvel
So what: Christmas 2026 IMAX box office rides on Dune 3 rather than a Marvel tentpole — a concentrated, checkable catalyst — and the refusal signals IMAX will trade near-term revenue for filmmaker loyalty that locks in future slots.
“we love you guys and we love to do your movie but we have a commitment and that's the currency that we're made of” ▶ 6:51
Filmmakers now approach IMAX directly before a distributor is attached — Jerry Bruckheimer and Joseph Kosinski locked an IMAX date for F1 more than a year out and only then went to Warner Bros.
causal🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXF1Jerry BruckheimerJoseph KosinskiWarner BrosApple
So what: Being the first call rather than the last gives IMAX first pick of dates and screens, and shows the moat is talent relationships, not just hardware — hard for a repackaged large-format competitor to replicate.
The Odyssey: box office proof points and indexing 5
Through 24 days The Odyssey did roughly $461 million domestic versus $264 million for Oppenheimer over the same window, a comparison the hosts attribute largely to IMAX pre-sales and Nolan shooting the entire film on IMAX cameras.
data_point🗣 host (Michael)conviction: highIMAXThe OdysseyOppenheimerChristopher Nolan
So what: A ~75% uplift over the prior Nolan benchmark is the cleanest available evidence that IMAX-native production materially expands a film's total gross, which is the argument for higher IMAX take rates.
The Odyssey will be near $300 million of IMAX box office with another $40 million already in pre-sales and a run through September, so roughly $340 million is effectively booked before another ticket is sold.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXThe Odyssey
So what: A single title contributing well over $300M of IMAX gross reframes the per-film ceiling and directly supports full-year guidance; the $40M pre-sale backlog is a near-riskless revenue line.
IMAX's own film team modeled an 'ultimate' of $190 million for The Odyssey while Gelfond argued for over $300 million — and he says even his own forecast was too low, implying internal forecasting systematically understates IMAX-native event films.
contrarian🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXThe Odyssey
So what: If IMAX's internal ultimates run low on event titles, sell-side models anchored to company guidance are likely conservative on the biggest releases — an upside-surprise pattern to watch on Dune 3.
“at IMAX, we had an ultimate of 190 for the movie. That's what my film team thought it would do. And I said it would do over 300.” ▶ 17:46
IMAX indexed around 26% of The Odyssey's box office and has exceeded 30% on some titles, but Gelfond frames indexing as a function of how many screens a film opens on and what regular theaters are doing rather than a number to maximize.
framework🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: mediumIMAXThe Odyssey
So what: Indexing is the key per-film revenue metric; understanding it caps out near 26-30% on wide releases sets a realistic ceiling for modeling IMAX's share of any single blockbuster.
Gelfond says the required input for an IMAX title's success is the director leaning in during pre-production, not the technology — citing Nolan's 15-item demand list (including the sound-dampening 'blimp' and global spare-parts depots), Ryan Coogler's aspect-ratio explainer for Sinners, and Villeneuve on Dune 3.
framework🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXChristopher NolanRyan CooglerSinnersDenis VilleneuveDune 3
So what: A usable screen for which upcoming IMAX titles will overperform: check whether the director publicly frames the film as IMAX-native, rather than counting screens.
“it's not just the technology and you need the filmmaker to say this is the way I made it and this is what the way they're thinking about IMAX as they're like in pre-production for the film” ▶ 29:46
Untapped monetization: merch, ticket pricing, film prints 4
IMAX started merchandising only this year and demand overwhelmed a deliberately conservative rollout: 2,500 IMAX-camera popcorn buckets sold in two hours, another 2,500 in under an hour, 2,500 in China in 60 seconds, and 20,000 Odyssey film strips in 90 minutes.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXThe Odyssey
So what: A brand-monetization line starting from near zero with instant sell-throughs is a high-margin, non-box-office revenue stream not yet in most models — watch for a scaled merch program around Dune 3.
“So, we put 2500 on sale and they sold in 2 hours.” ▶ 14:05
IMAX does not set ticket prices and is being underpriced by its largest exhibitor: AMC in New York charges the same price for an IMAX film ticket as a regular ticket and sells it at half price on Wednesdays (~$14), while Brendan Theaters in Las Vegas charges $50 for IMAX film and sells out.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXAMCAdam AronBrendan Theaters
So what: A ~3.5x price gap between exhibitors on identical product is pure captured-value upside if exhibitors adopt dynamic or premium pricing — an underappreciated option on IMAX's per-screen economics.
“AMC here in New York is charging the same price for an IMAX film ticket as they are for a regular ticket.” ▶ 14:52
Film (versus digital) IMAX releases carry higher ticket prices, higher utilization and higher indexing, but each 70mm print costs about $50,000 and the projectors — built roughly 50 years ago and scavenged rather than manufactured — cost more, so film can only be deployed in very high-traffic locations.
framework🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAX70mm film
So what: Explains why IMAX cannot simply expand its highest-margin format on demand; print and projector economics, not demand, are the binding constraint on the premium tier.
“So, one print at one theater, so think of the one that shows at 68th Street here in Manhattan, cost $50,000.” ▶ 22:43
The 70mm film footprint has grown from 30 theaters at Oppenheimer to 41 globally (around 25 in the US), roughly four IMAX film titles are slated for next year, and Gelfond says an unproven new idea could add about 10 more — with the LA Live conversion done January 1 doing $800,000 in its first six months.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: mediumIMAX70mm filmLA LiveOppenheimer
So what: A ~37% increase in the highest-indexing screen base since Oppenheimer, plus a possible further 25% step-up, is a quantifiable driver of per-title IMAX gross on the 2027 slate.
Non-Hollywood content and box-office lumpiness 3
IMAX operates 800 theaters in China and released Ne Zha there, a film Gelfond notes the US audience has not heard of that grossed about $1.2 billion — evidence IMAX's economics are not solely a bet on Hollywood output.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXChinaNe Zha
So what: China local-language blockbusters can offset a weak Hollywood slate year, diversifying the single biggest perceived risk in the IMAX story.
IMAX's local-language and international film business has compounded at a 24% CAGR from 2019 to 2025, spanning anime released worldwide rather than only in Japan and a big Indian title being opened globally next — titles no theater chain is otherwise playing.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXanimeJapanChinaIndia
So what: A 24% CAGR segment inside a company priced on Hollywood tentpole risk is the most direct rebuttal to the 'lumpy slate' bear case — and it is largely uncontested white space.
“So 24% Kager since from 2019 to 2025.” ▶ 24:29
IMAX programmed 120 pieces of content last year including concerts (a live Stop Making Sense event with David Byrne from Toronto), NBA Finals in China, NHL tests in Europe, an HBO series and an Interstellar re-release that did over $5 million — but marketing cost, not availability, limits how many catalog titles can be re-released, so only iconic titles work.
framework🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXInterstellarNBANHLHBODavid Byrne
So what: Sets the realistic size of the alternative-content filler business: it smooths box-office gaps only for titles that can carry a standalone marketing spend, so don't model unlimited catalog monetization.
Forward visibility: backlog, guidance and the 2027-28 slate 3
Gelfond argues IMAX has more forward visibility than almost any business because it publicly guides on a theater installation backlog and has films contractually committed into 2028 and some into 2029, including all four Sam Mendes Beatles films for Sony in 2028 and a Bond title.
thesis🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXSam MendesThe BeatlesSonyJames Bond
So what: Directly attacks the 'unpredictable 2027-28' discount the hosts say Wall Street applies; the theater backlog and film commitments are disclosed and checkable against that discount.
“I I would argue that we have more visibility into the future than almost any other business cuz if you're a car company, you have zero sales on opening day.” ▶ 9:38
The 2027 IMAX slate discussed includes The Legend of Zelda (Nintendo IP, whose director cold-called Gelfond a year ahead insisting on IMAX), a new Ryan Gosling Star Wars film, Frozen 3, Minecraft, Shrek 5, The Mummy and Great Beyond, with the Avengers sequel at Christmas the following year — and Gelfond notes few movies were made in 2026 while many were made for 2027.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: mediumIMAXThe Legend of ZeldaNintendoStar WarsRyan GoslingFrozen 3MinecraftShrek 5
So what: A dense, mostly pre-signed 2027 slate against a thin 2026 production year argues against the feared post-Odyssey air pocket in 2027 revenue.
“this year a lot of movies weren't made. Next year a lot of movies were made. So I think we're going to do very well.” ▶ 39:43
IMAX guided to $1.2 billion in global box office last year and came in around $1.3 billion; this year it guided to $1.4 billion and Gelfond says the company is roughly $300 million away with seven months of results behind it.
data_point🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAX
So what: A guide-and-beat track record plus a specific run-rate against the current target gives a concrete checkpoint for whether 2026 guidance is again conservative.
Valuation gap, M&A posture and industry structure 7
The hosts argue IMAX's brand-to-market-cap ratio is one of the most lopsided in the stock market — the stock was pitched at $17 on May 14, 2024 at roughly a $1 billion cap and now sits near $3 billion — and that Wall Street's remaining discount reflects dislike of uncertainty about which films come out in 2027 and 2028 rather than the current business.
thesis🗣 host (Michael)conviction: highIMAXWall Street
So what: Frames the bull case precisely: the re-rating from ~$1B to ~$3B has happened, so incremental upside depends on closing the slate-uncertainty discount, which the backlog disclosures can do.
“the amount of influence and brand recognition that you have compared to your market cap was a joke. I thought it was very lopsided at a billion dollars.” ▶ 8:55
NRG brand studies place IMAX's brand on the level of Disney and Nike, and Gelfond concedes the company gets very little recognition of that brand value in relative monetization — which is the explicit rationale for merchandising, licensing and the automotive AV deal.
thesis🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXNRGDisneyNike
So what: Management is openly naming the brand-monetization gap as the value-creation lever, so new non-theatrical revenue lines are the metric to track for a re-rating.
“Well, we've done brand studies with NRG and our brand is on a level of Disney and Nike and people like that.” ▶ 19:53
Gelfond contrasts IMAX's asset-light retrofit model with Sphere, which he says cost close to $3 billion for one venue — he says he would not own the stock, does not buy on narrative the way he passed on SpaceX, and invests on earnings and cash flow, while conceding Sphere's move to licensing is a better model than building each venue.
contrarian🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highSphere EntertainmentIMAXSpaceX
So what: The comparison the hosts raise — a globally scaled brand that can convert existing theaters versus a $3B single-venue capex model — is the core relative-value argument for IMAX over premium-venue peers.
“I personally, I wouldn't own that stock. I didn't buy SpaceX. I don't put my finger in the air and say, "Hey, this is worth $2 trillion." Not the way I invest. I like looking at earnings and cash flow.” ▶ 20:52
On takeover speculation Gelfond says people have been kicking the tires over a very long period, IMAX is not actively seeking a buyer, and the best thing that happened to the company was not selling 10 years ago — but he will talk at a blowaway price.
thesis🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: mediumIMAXBloomberg
So what: There is no live process, so IMAX should not be owned for a near-term takeout; management's stated bar is a premium price, which caps M&A optionality as a thesis leg.
“the best thing that's happened to this company is that it didn't sell 10 years ago because we've said we have so much promise in it and we run it to maximize shareholder value” ▶ 42:04
Gelfond refuses to call the Paramount-Warner situation good or bad, arguing prognosticators have almost always been wrong about the movie business, and notes the offsetting positives: streamers as a large new content source, David Ellison's promise of 30 movies a year and a newly announced 45-day theatrical window.
contrarian🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: mediumParamountWarner BrosDavid EllisonNetflixUniversal
So what: A 45-day window plus 30 films a year from a consolidated studio is a volume tailwind for exhibition that partly offsets losing Warner Bros as an independent buyer of content.
“You know, the prognosticators, if I could say one thing about them, they've almost always been wrong about the future of the movie business.” ▶ 32:04
Streaming platforms have become among IMAX's best content partners — Apple's F1 did $100 million in IMAX and $600 million overall as Apple's biggest release, Amazon's Hail Mary also did $100 million in IMAX, and Netflix is giving David Fincher's Cliff Booth (written by Tarantino, starring Brad Pitt) an exclusive IMAX theatrical run between Thanksgiving and Christmas before streaming.
thesis🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highIMAXAppleF1AmazonHail MaryNetflixDavid FincherQuentin TarantinoBrad Pitt
So what: Streamers using IMAX exclusivity to eventize launches converts the historic streaming threat into a new demand pool; the Netflix Cliff Booth exclusive is the near-term test case.
Disney's Infinity Vision is a marketing bundle of existing branded screens rather than newly built large-format theaters, launched because Disney could not get IMAX screens for Avengers, and Gelfond says he is not losing sleep over it.
contrarian🗣 Rich Gelfond, IMAX CEOconviction: highDisneyInfinity VisionIMAXAvengers
So what: The most obvious competitive threat to the IMAX moat is, per the CEO, repackaged existing capacity — worth watching whether consumers price Infinity Vision tickets like IMAX or like a regular screen.
“But Infinity Vision is a marketing technique that's they're not building new theaters. They're taking existing theaters with an X in them and they're bundling them and they're calling it Infinity Vision.” ▶ 7:36

a16z Podcast 1 episode(s) · 25 insights

Kavak's Playbook for Rebuilding a Company Around AI37 min25 insights2026-08-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Ali Masa, head of AI at Latin American used-car marketplace Kavak, details how the company scrapped its multi-agent workflow architecture after Opus 4.5 shipped and rebuilt around one long-running agent per customer, each in its own virtual machine, instantiating 100,000-200,000 agents daily. He gives hard operating numbers - 96% of interactions and 95% of transactions agent-handled, agents converting 2.1x better than the human sales team, NPS tripled, car loans approved in under three minutes, warranties down ~20-26% - plus an AI-CEO experiment running a Mexican city's P&L that delivered 1.5x profit growth in its first month. His investment frame: spend as much on evals as on agents, tier your token spend by measurable ROI, and expect net-new AI-native companies rather than incumbents to capture the value.
Agent-per-customer architecture · 4Agent deployment economics and scale · 4Evals as the throttle · 3Agents selling and underwriting high-ticket regulated products · 4Org redesign: Jedi Academy, flat teams, and an AI CEO · 5Creative destruction and the incumbent adoption gap · 5
Agent-per-customer architecture 4
Kavak designed its current architecture by asking what the company would look like if built in 2035 with GPT-10-level intelligence, then building that today rather than retrofitting AI onto the existing org.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakGPTAI agents
So what: A reusable diligence question for any AI-transformation story: ask whether management designed backwards from future model capability or forwards from today's org chart.
“The questions we ask ourselves is how would we build Kabak in 2035 with fable 10 or or or GPT 10 level intelligence and actually that company looks very different than than what we had built” ▶ 3:28
Kavak spawns a dedicated long-running agent per customer with its own virtual machine, persistent memory of years of interactions, a CLI with access to every internal tool and API, and a long-term goal of maximizing that customer's lifetime value.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakAI agentsvirtual machines
So what: Per-customer agent instances with dedicated VMs imply a compute and orchestration bill that scales with customer count, not query count - a distinct infrastructure demand curve from chat-style deployments.
“it's it's a virtual machine with an agent with access to memory and evals and the CLI where they can access every tool and every API in my company and the long-term goal” ▶ 29:55
Kavak destroyed two years of working multi-agent graph and workflow infrastructure - the systems that had carried it to profitability and growth - after Opus 4.5 shipped, on the judgment that scaffolding now constrains rather than augments frontier model intelligence.
thesis🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakOpus 4.5multi-agent systemsagentic workflows
So what: Signals that agent-orchestration tooling and graph frameworks face obsolescence risk with each model generation - a durability question for startups selling that layer.
“But then Opus 4.5 came out and I realized like this isn't the right paradigm anymore. like the the intelligence now doesn't need like the graph and the multi- aent” ▶ 29:10
Masa advises builders to skip agentic workflows entirely and go straight to long-running agents with hard objectives, arguing that workflow-and-graph architectures are the wrong starting point rather than a necessary stepping stone.
contrarian🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highAI agentsagentic workflows
So what: Directly against the prevailing enterprise pattern of starting with narrow workflow automation; a fast-follower argument for greenfield teams.
“like if I could advise everyone don't build agentic workflows to graphs or or functions or or objectives” ▶ 28:44
Agent deployment economics and scale 4
Kavak instantiates between 100,000 and 200,000 agents per day, with individual agents running anywhere from three minutes to eight hours to three days before setting an alarm for their next task and sleeping.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakAI agents
So what: One of the few disclosed production agent-fleet counts; sets a real-world benchmark for compute demand per mid-size consumer business.
“Every day between a 100 and 200,000 agents get instantiated in a day. They wake up, they work sometimes for three minutes, sometimes for eight hours, sometimes for three days, and they like set an alarm clock for their next task and they” ▶ 8:30
Agents handle 96% of all customer interactions and 95% of all transactions at Kavak, with humans remaining only at physical car handover.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakAI agents
So what: Demonstrates end-to-end agent handling of high-ticket regulated commerce is already in production, not pilot - raises the bar for what 'AI adoption' should mean in comps.
“So so to give you a sense of the scale 90 like 96% of all interactions uh are handled by agents. So so no humans there. Um like 95% of all transactions are completely handled by by agents.” ▶ 8:04
Masa says he is now investing more in tokens than in knowledge workers, treating inference spend as a direct substitute for headcount budget.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavaktokensknowledge workers
So what: A concrete instance of opex shifting from payroll to compute - the substitution that underwrites inference-demand and labor-displacement theses.
“I'm investing more today in tokens than in knowledge workers.” ▶ 0:00
Masa grades token spend in three tiers: tier three tokens go to production agents with directly measurable per-token ROI, tier two are dev-tool tokens measurable only indirectly, and tier one is ungoverned employee use of chat and coding tools with no visibility into value.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: hightokensAI coding toolsROI
So what: A practical screen for whether an enterprise's rising AI spend is compounding or burning - ask which tier the incremental dollars sit in.
“level tier three tokens the most valuable are this agents where you can get the ROI of each specific token and I can do that now. That's great news for me because I'm growing and because I know the ROI of each token” ▶ 27:09
Evals as the throttle 3
Kavak spends roughly the same engineer time, tokens and money building evals as building the agents themselves, treating eval quality as the brake that determines maximum safe deployment speed.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakevalsAI agents
So what: A 1:1 eval-to-agent spend ratio is a concrete diligence benchmark and a sizable, underappreciated line item in agent TCO.
“we spend about the same amount of time, engineer time, tokens, and and and money on building the evils than building the agents” ▶ 9:30
Masa inverts the usual risk posture: rather than going slow because evals are weak, he asks how fast the company can go given eval quality, arguing companies that move slowly do so because they lack brakes, not because caution is correct.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highevalsAI deployment
So what: Reframes AI governance spend as a speed enabler rather than a compliance cost - the argument enterprise eval vendors need to win budget.
“I like to move extremely fast, but in order to move fast, you need to have brakes, right? Imagine a car. Uh you'll hit on the gas just if you have the right brakes.” ▶ 9:03
Kavak evaluates agents on business outcomes - did the customer convert, get the loan approved, re-engage later - and Masa says companies measuring call counts or call minutes are tracking superficial KPIs that break precisely where it matters.
causal🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakevalsconversionKPIs
So what: Outcome-linked evals are the tell separating real agent deployments from demo-grade ones when assessing AI-services and agent vendors.
“Like the important thing is did this customer convert? Is it bringing value to the customer and is the customer happy to re-engage with us after a while?” ▶ 10:19
Agents selling and underwriting high-ticket regulated products 4
Kavak never built customer support agents - it built sales agents first, deliberately pointing AI at the highest-leverage revenue task rather than the lowest-risk one.
contrarian🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakAI sales agentscustomer support
So what: Cuts against the market's assumption that customer service is the entry wedge for enterprise AI; sales-agent budgets may be the larger pool.
“So so we never built customer support or customer service agents. We we built like sales agents.”
Kavak's sales agent converts 2.1x better than its human sales team, up from 50% better at launch, while tripling NPS and customer satisfaction scores.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakAI sales agentsNPSconversion
So what: A rare disclosed head-to-head on agent versus human sales productivity, and the improving trend line matters more than the level.
“We tripled uh NPS and customer satisfaction score by putting the the agent in front of the of the customer. And it at first it converted like 50% more than our human team and now it's converting over that like 2.1 uh x more.” ▶ 12:23
Kavak approves car loans in under three minutes in markets where the norm is two months or more, and its vertical integration lets a struggling borrower return the car and swap into a cheaper one with lower payments rather than default.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakMexicoauto lendingfintech
So what: Vertical integration plus agent underwriting is a credible loss-mitigation mechanism in thin-file emerging-market consumer credit - relevant to LatAm fintech credit models.
“usually in in Mexico and and in some emerging markets it'll get like two months or or or or more to get a car loan approved. Um, we usually approve it in under three minutes.”
Kavak reframed itself from a transactional business measuring cars bought and sold to a relational one where agents are assigned to most of its 10 million database customers, and Masa estimates activating just 1% of that base is worth hundreds of millions of dollars.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakcustomer lifetime valueAI agents
So what: Quantifies the dormant-base monetization thesis: agents make previously uneconomic long-tail customer relationships worth working.
“now I have 10 million customers in my database and I have agents assigned to most of them with the task of maximizing their lifetime value. Now, we're selling cars and and and personal loans and very high ticket items.” ▶ 6:49
Org redesign: Jedi Academy, flat teams, and an AI CEO 5
Kavak carved out one Mexican city as an AI CEO experiment, putting an agent in a harness with authority over that city's P&L; it missed its first-month goal of doubling profits but delivered 1.5x, roughly 50% more profit, while improving customer satisfaction, inventory rotation and financing penetration.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakCuernavacaMexicoAI CEO
So what: The clearest datapoint yet that agentic management, not just agentic labor, produces P&L movement - and it had only been running six weeks.
“It didn't reach it but it was 1.5x like 50% more profits.” ▶ 16:59
Kavak runs an internal six-week training program called the Jedi Academy that Masa designed and leads himself, taking everyone from the CEO to mechanics to AI engineers through to shipping state-of-the-art agents to production, and it is continuously rewritten because external programs cannot keep pace.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakJedi Academyworkforce retraining
So what: In-house, continuously refreshed AI training is emerging as a required capability - and a headwind for traditional executive-education and enterprise-training providers.
“we train everyone and after six weeks they launch state-of-the-art uh agents AI agents to production and it's mechanics and and finance guys and engineers is like everyone can do it” ▶ 21:02
Kavak's org is now flat, senior, cross-functional teams that either build agents, work for agents, or operate in the physical world in front of customers - with agents sometimes acting as the boss of humans.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakorg designmiddle management
So what: A concrete template for the post-middle-management org; the three-way role taxonomy is a usable lens for assessing headcount plans.
“any process it's really a collaboration of agents and humans and sometimes like agents are the bosses or of of humans and sometimes humans are designing the agents” ▶ 22:14
Kavak inverted the human-in-the-loop pattern: instead of escalating stuck cases to a tier-2 queue where the loop is never closed and no training data is generated, its agents call an API to request help and a human answers, keeping the agent as case owner.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakhuman-in-the-loopAI agentstraining data
So what: Escalation design determines whether a deployment compounds or plateaus - a specific technical detail to probe in any agent-vendor diligence.
“if an agent hits a wall or cancels something, it'll call this API saying I need help. And on the other side, it's not an agent or software, it's a human helping them out.” ▶ 24:54
Kavak still hires and trains humans for physical-world roles, retaining around 800 mechanics in Mexico, and instead ships them an AI sidekick called El Mike built on the same harness that guides inspections - cutting warranty claims by roughly 20-26% while speeding inspection and repair.
data_point🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highKavakMexicoEl Mikemechanicswarranties
So what: Marks the current capability boundary - dexterity and physical senses - and quantifies augmentation value where substitution is not yet possible.
“warranties came down around like 20 26% since we since we launched and customer satisfaction again went up” ▶ 19:10
Creative destruction and the incumbent adoption gap 5
Masa argues AI transformation must be top-down with leadership holding a specific three-to-five-year picture of the company, and that hackathon-and-use-case sponsorship programs - the dominant enterprise approach - simply do not work.
contrarian🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highenterprise AI adoptioncorporate strategy
So what: If bottom-up adoption programs are structurally ineffective, enterprise AI revenue concentrates in vendors selling to CEOs, not to innovation teams.
“It's like be very clear on what the company will look like in three or five years and then start building that and be like very vertical in in guiding your troops towards that.” ▶ 26:20
Masa invokes Schumpeterian creative destruction to argue value from AI will accrue to net-new companies destroying incumbents, not to incumbents adopting AI, because no CEO of a large public company will credibly bet on demolishing forty years of built assets.
thesis🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highJoseph Schumpetercreative destructionincumbentsstartups
So what: A direct argument for venture exposure to AI-native challengers over incumbent AI-adoption stories in disrupted verticals.
“it's really hard for a CEO today, especially of a large company or public company, to go and say, "Hey, like I'm betting everything on AI. Uh the company has to look this way.” ▶ 32:20
Masa uses the electrification lag as the base rate: Edison's efficient dynamo and the enabling technologies existed by 1879-1881, yet factories that merely swapped a coal engine for an electric one got about 6% efficiency, while redesigning around distributed small dynamos on a flat greenfield site delivered roughly 3x productivity - a forty-year gap before Ford.
framework🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highThomas EdisonHenry Fordelectrificationproductivity
So what: Sets the expected shape of AI productivity diffusion: a long lag, then step-change gains concentrated in redesigned firms - implying near-term aggregate productivity data will disappoint.
“you could have built Ford's factory 40 years before Ford. The technology was there, everything was there.” ▶ 33:28
Masa predicts superficial AI adoption delivers 6-10% improvement rather than 10x, framing the gap as the innovator's dilemma at industrial scale.
prediction🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highenterprise AI adoptioninnovator's dilemma
So what: A quantified expectation to test against reported AI ROI in incumbent earnings calls - single-digit gains confirm the thesis rather than refute it.
“in the end, that'll give you a 6% or a 10% improvement, not a 10x improvement. And it's like the innovator's dilemma at an industrial uh scale.” ▶ 34:29
Masa argues the right target for recursive self-improvement is the organization rather than the model, because economic value over 4,000 years has been delivered by organizations, not individuals - so the compounding loop to build is a self-improving company that continuously absorbs newer models.
thesis🗣 Ali Masa (head of AI, Kavak)conviction: highrecursive self-improvementorganizational designAI models
So what: Shifts the RSI debate from lab capability to firm-level architecture - the exponential to underwrite is in company value creation, not model IQ.
“economic value in humanity for for the past 4,000 years has been delivered by organization, not not by individuals. So what you want to self-improve and to engage in that loop is the organization that can deliver more economic value.” ▶ 30:30
